FOREWORD







The field of Reinforcement Learning (RL) has witnessed remarkable growth and transformation over the past few decades, evolving from a niche area of artificial intelligence into a cornerstone of modern machine learning and AI research. This book, titled "Reinforcement Learning: Foundations and Applications," is a testament to this evolution, offering both a deep dive into the theoretical underpinnings of RL and a broad survey of its practical applications.

As we stand on the cusp of a new era in artificial intelligence, the importance of reinforcement learning cannot be overstated. Its unique approach, where agents learn to make decisions by interacting with their environment, mirrors the learning processes seen in nature. This paradigm has not only driven breakthroughs in gaming, robotics, and autonomous systems but also opened new avenues in fields as diverse as healthcare, finance, and supply chain management.

The contributors to this volume include leading experts and pioneers in the field. Their collective insights provide readers with a comprehensive understanding of RL, from the basic principles to the latest advancements. The chapters are meticulously curated to ensure that both newcomers and seasoned practitioners will find valuable knowledge and inspiration.

For students and researchers, this book serves as an essential guide to mastering the core concepts and staying abreast of the latest research trends. For professionals and industry practitioners, it offers a wealth of practical knowledge and real-world case studies that illustrate the transformative potential of RL technologies.

As you embark on this intellectual journey through "Reinforcement Learning: Foundations and Applications," I encourage you to not only absorb the wealth of information contained within these pages but also to think creatively about how RL can be applied to solve the complex challenges we face today and in the future. The power of reinforcement learning lies in its ability to adapt, learn, and improve traits that are vital as we strive to create more intelligent and responsive systems.

In closing, I extend my deepest gratitude to the authors, editors, and reviewers who have contributed to this seminal work. Their dedication and expertise have culminated in a book that I am confident will become a key reference in the field of reinforcement learning. May this book inspire you, challenge you, and ultimately, equip you with the tools and knowledge to contribute to the ongoing advancement of artificial intelligence.

Pawan Kumar
Department of Science and Technology
WISE-KIRAN Division
New Delhi
India

PREFACE







Over the past decade, Reinforcement Learning (RL) has evolved from a niche area of artificial intelligence to a pivotal component in the landscape of modern machine learning and autonomous systems. As we stand on the brink of this technological revolution, it is imperative to both reflect on the foundational principles that have guided us and explore the innovative applications that are propelling us forward. The book titled "Reinforcement Learning: Foundations and Applications" aims to serve as a comprehensive guide, bridging the gap between theoretical underpinnings and practical implementations of RL.

The foundations of reinforcement learning are built on the principles of trial and error, reward and punishment, and the pursuit of optimal policies. These principles are not just academic constructs but are deeply rooted in behavioural psychology and neuroscience, offering a rich interdisciplinary dimension to the study of RL. This book begins with an exploration of these fundamental concepts, providing readers with a robust understanding of the mathematical and conceptual frameworks that underpin reinforcement learning.

However, understanding the theory is only part of the journey. The real-world applications of RL are where the magic happens. From autonomous vehicles navigating complex environments to intelligent agents mastering intricate games, the potential of RL to transform industries is boundless. Each chapter in this book delves into specific applications, showcasing how RL is being used to solve some of the most challenging problems across various domains. These case studies not only illustrate the versatility of RL but also provide practical insights into the challenges and solutions encountered in real-world scenarios.

The journey of creating this edited volume has been both enlightening and inspiring. We have had the privilege of collaborating with leading researchers and practitioners in the field, whose contributions have enriched the book with diverse perspectives and cutting-edge knowledge. Their expertise spans a wide array of disciplines, reflecting the interdisciplinary nature of RL and its far-reaching impact.

We envision this book as a valuable resource for a broad audience. For students and newcomers, it offers a thorough introduction to the principles and practices of RL. For researchers and practitioners, it serves as a reference that highlights both established methods and emerging trends. Ultimately, our goal is to foster a deeper understanding and appreciation of RL, inspiring readers to contribute to the ongoing advancement of this dynamic field.

As you embark on this exploration of reinforcement learning, we hope you find the content as stimulating and rewarding as we have found in bringing it together. May this book serve as a foundation for your own discoveries and innovations in the world of reinforcement learning.

Exploring the Basics of Reinforcement Learning



Punam Rattan1, *, Ram Krishnan Raji Nair1, Korhan Cengiz2
1 School of Computer Application, Lovely Professional University, Phagwara, Punjab, India
2 Department of Information Technologies, Faculty of Informatics and Management, University of Hradec Kralove, Kralove, Czech Republic

Abstract

Sequential decisions, or decisions that are made repeatedly over time, like the daily stock replenishment decisions made by inventory control, can be optimized using a type of learning model called reinforcement learning. Reinforcement Learning (RL) and human learning are comparable in that people can learn abilities that enhance their performance in challenging tasks, such as test-taking, gymnastics, and swimming. These human action skills often inspire RL. More specifically, in terms of real-world applications, the objective is to determine the best method for managing uncertainty while making successive decisions over time in a dynamic system. A policy is a plan to make future decisions consistently over time in a dynamic system. The purpose of RL was to determine the best way for a dynamic system to function in various conditions. This first chapter has covered the basic ideas behind reinforcement learning. We delve into great detail on the numerous nuances, characteristics, and challenges of reinforcement learning.

Keywords: Artificial intelligence, Deep Q-network, Machine learning, Natural language processing, Reinforcement learning.

* Corresponding author Punam Rattan: School of Computer Application, Lovely Professional University, Phagwara, Punjab, India; E-mail: punamrattan@gmail.com

INTRODUCTION

RL, a branch of Machine Learning (ML), employs trial-and-error methods to maximize the rewards acquired collectively based on the feedback received for individual actions, enabling Artificial Intelligence (AI) based systems to function in a dynamic environment. One subfield of ML is reinforcement learning. To maximize the benefits, it involves acting appropriately in a particular circumstance. Many software programs and gadgets use it to determine the best course of action or behavior in a specific situation. In contrast to supervised learning, which involves training the model with the correct answer pre-existing in the training set, RL involves training the model in the absence of an answer and

using the reinforcement agent's judgment to determine how to accomplish the given task [1]. Even in the absence of a training dataset, it will eventually learn from its encounters.

The idea that a positive reward reinforces an optimal behaviour or action lies at the heart of reinforcement learning. RL algorithms are used by machines and software agents to determine the optimal behaviour based on feedback from the environment. Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms have the ability to continuously adapt to their environment over time, depending on the complexity of the task, with the objective of maximizing cumulative rewards. Thus, like the unsteady child, a robot learning to walk through RL will attempt several approaches to reach the goal, receive feedback regarding the effectiveness of those approaches, and then modify until walking is the desired outcome. For example, the robot falls when it takes a large step forward, so it modifies its step to become smaller to determine if that is the key to remaining upright. Through various iterations, it keeps learning and eventually gains the ability to walk. In this instance, standing tall is the prize while falling is the penalty. Optimal actions are encouraged by the robot based on the feedback it receives for its activities [2]. RL algorithms are used to evaluate data and choose the optimal course of action. After each action, the algorithm receives feedback to help it determine if the choice it made was neutral, incorrect, or correct. It is a helpful technique for automated systems that must make numerous small decisions without human intervention [3]. RL is a self-governing, self-teaching system that essentially gains knowledge by error. It acts with the goal of optimizing rewards, or, to put it another way, it learns by doing to get the best outcomes.

RL works in a mathematical framework consisting of the following components as shown in Fig. (1):

Fig. (1))
Reinforcement process [4].
  1. State Space: All available information and problem features that are useful for making a decision. This includes fully known or measured variables (for example, the current levels of stock on hand in inventory control) as well as unmeasured variables for which you might only have a belief or estimate (for example, a forecast of demand for the future day or week).
  2. Action Space: Decisions that one can take in each state of the system.
  3. Reward Signal: A scalar signal that provides the necessary feedback about performance, and, therefore, the opportunity to learn which actions are beneficial in any given state. Learning is both local in its nature, providing immediate as well as long-term gains, because actions taken in any state lead to future states where another action is taken, and so on.

BASIC IDEAS IN REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

The following components are present in every RL problem, as shown in Fig. (2).

Fig. (2))
Components of the reinforcement process [5].
  1. Agent: The program in charge of the object of interest (a robot, for example) is called an agent.
  2. Environment: This is a programming definition of the external world. The environment consists of everything that the agent or agents interact with. It's designed to give the impression that it is a real-world case for the agent [6]. It is necessary to demonstrate an agent's performance, or whether it will function properly in an actual application.
  3. Rewards: This provides us with a score indicating the algorithm's performance in relation to the surroundings. It is shown as either 0 or 1. A “1” indicates that the policy network made the proper decision, a “0” indicates that it made a bad one. Put differently, earnings and losses are represented by rewards.
  4. Policy: The policy is the algorithm that the agent uses to choose what to do. This is the section that may use models or not.

ONLINE VS. OFFLINE LEARNING

An agent can get data for learning rules in two broad ways: online and offline, as shown in Fig. (3).

Fig. (3))
Online vs. offline reinforcement learning
  1. Online: In this case, data are directly collected by an agent through interaction with its environment. Iteratively processing and gathering this data occurs while the agent is still engaging with that environment [7].
  2. Offline: An agent can learn from logged data about an environment even if it does not have direct access to it. This refers to education that is conducted offline. A significant portion of research has focused on offline learning due to the practical challenges associated with directly interacting with surroundings to train models [8].

RL IMPLEMENTATION APPROACHES

RL can be applied in ML in three primary ways as follows:

  1. Value-based: The purpose of the value-based method was to identify the maximum value at a state under all policies. As a result, the agent anticipates the long-term return under policy π at any state or states. The optimum course of action for the largest potential future benefits is determined by this technique, which does not rely on the value function. The agent employs this technique to implement a policy in a manner that maximizes the reward at the end of each stage [9]. Using the policy-based approach, there are two main categories of policies. The same action is produced by every state's policy (π). Probabilistic Probability dictates the generated action in this method.
  2. Model-based, in this method, the agent investigates the environment to gain knowledge about it by building a virtual model of it. Since the model representation varies depending on the environment, there is no set solution or algorithm for this technique.

REINFORCEMENT TYPES

There are two types of reinforcement, namely Positive and Negative.

  1. Positive: The definition of positive reinforcement is when an action that results from a certain behaviour makes the behaviour stronger and more frequent. Put otherwise, it produces a favourable impact on behaviour. The benefits of this type of RL include optimizing performance and maintaining change over time [10]. However, excessive reinforcement may result in an overflow of states, which may reduce the effectiveness of the method.
  2. Negative: When a negative condition is avoided or halted, negative reinforcement strengthens the behaviour. Negative RL has the following benefits: it increases behaviour, defies performance standards, and supplies just enough to meet minimum behavioural requirements.

WORKING OF REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

The three primary components of an RL system are:

  1. The agent
  2. The environment
  3. The reward signal

The agent learns how to act based on its current state and the reward signal it gets from the environment. The environment directs the agent's actions and provides feedback in the form of reward signals. The reward signal is a scalar value that shows how well the agent is achieving its goal.

Different methodologies, such as actor-critic approaches, policy gradient methods, and Q-learning, can be used to train RL agents [11]. These algorithms employ various techniques to determine the expected cumulative reward and adjust the agent's policy accordingly. An agent and its performance environment are examples of reinforcement parameters used in ML. The learning system is made up of a few additional parts in addition to these two. The following are the elements of RL, as shown in Table 1.

Table 1 Elements of rl (policy, reward and value).
Policy Reward Function Value Function
The policy specifies the behaviour of the learning agent for a specific duration. It is a mapping of perceived environmental states to appropriate behaviours to take in those states. In an RL problem, a goal is defined by the reward function. A reward function is a function that assigns a score based on the environment's condition. Value functions define what is beneficial over the long term. The entire reward that an agent might anticipate earning over time, beginning from a certain state, is what determines the value of that state.

DIFFERENCE BETWEEN RL AND SUPERVISED LEARNING

The following Table 2 differentiates between RL and SL:

Table 2 Difference between RL and Supervised Learning.
RL SL
RL is based on sequential decision-making. To put it simply, the output of the previous input dictates the outcome of the succeeding input, and the condition of the present input determines the output. The choice in supervised learning is based on the first input, or the input provided at the beginning.
Since decisions in RL are contingent, we identify the orders in which the dependent decisions occur. Since each decision in supervised learning is made independently of the others, each decision is assigned a label.
Example Chess game, text summarization Example: Object recognition, spam detection

RL NEITHER SUPERVISED NOR UNSUPERVISED

One type of ML technique that defies classification as either supervised or unsupervised is reinforcement learning. It lies in the middle of the two. Because it needs tagged datasets, it cannot be categorized as a supervised algorithm. However, since its goal is to categorize data points, it cannot be categorized as an unsupervised algorithm. The fundamental notion behind applying RL is that it must use the idea of trial and error to rectify the actions made in various settings. It has been trained using datasets that depict actual events. It chooses a few actions based on them, for which it is rewarded or punished [12]. The goal was to optimize the gain to the greatest extent feasible. Applications for RL are numerous and include autonomous vehicles, robots, and gaming. It is also applied to complicated issues in energy management, healthcare, and finance.

RL VERSUS ML

To better comprehend reinforcement learning, it is helpful to compare it with conventional supervised ML. The goal of supervised ML is to make predictions about unknowns. The statistical presumptions of independent and identically distributed input data (input data) are optimal for its operation. This means that every input data record is a realization of a single, shared underlying input data distribution model, and that every input data record is believed to be independent of every other record. The supervised prediction model, when applied as a decision-making model, is a one-shot decision or prediction that is decoupled from all other input data records and solely concentrates on a specific record of input data.

With prediction accuracy maximization as the learning goal, the prediction model is learned under supervision. For every input record of data that is utilized for training, we may obtain the ground truth prediction. The paradigm for learning-based decision-making offered by reward learning, on the other hand, replaces a table of equally matched facts with ground truth labels. Rather, you have “current state-action-next state-reward” combinations of serially interconnected tuple trajectories; in other words, the data are no longer a static tabular data set as in supervised ML [13]. Building a policy, or a mapping or a strategy that computes the next best action to perform, is the aim of learning. This is because you know that every action you take influences future inputs into the policy mapping to compute the next-to-next best action, and so on. Thus, learning involves not just the current state but also the longer-term impacts on states that occur from the current behaviour in question. Consider inventory control as an instructive example, where a shop must decide from a supplier on weekly replenishment under unknown lead times for both supply and demand. The following could be included in the state-space: the current stock at the end of each week; demand observed over the previous few weeks to identify patterns in demand; previous replenishment actions taken over the previous few weeks to account for supplier lead time unpredictability; and an estimated demand forecast for the upcoming week. This includes interest-related estimated belief variables (such as forecasts) in addition to directly measurable and known variables [14]. A replenishment order placed at the end of each week to be received with some lead time could be included in the action space. Ultimately, the system's uncertainty relates to the lead times of suppliers and demand. Since stock levels in this example fluctuate due to uncertainty, you may impose limitations that punish overshooting or undershooting of certain levels.

KEY BENEFITS OF RL

RL has a lot of benefits, as shown in Fig. (4). The following three do, however, frequently stick out.

Fig. (4))
Example of reinforcement learning.
  1. Performs Well in Complicated Environments: RL algorithms work well in settings with a number of rules and dependencies. Even with superior environmental knowledge, a human might not be able to choose the optimum course of action in the same setting [15]. Alternatively, model-free RL algorithms swiftly adjust to dynamic settings and discover novel ways to maximize outcomes.
  2. Requires Less Human Intervention: In conventional ML algorithms, the system is guided by humans who must label data pairs. Using an RL algorithm eliminates the need for this. It picks up knowledge on its own. Simultaneously, it provides methods for incorporating human input, enabling algorithms to adjust to human corrections, expertise, and preferences.
  3. Aims for Long-term Objectives: RL is suited for situations where decisions have long-term effects because it is primarily concerned with maximizing long-term rewards. Because it can learn from delayed rewards, it is especially well-suited for real-world scenarios where feedback is not provided right away for every step. Long-term effects could result from choices made about energy storage or use, for instance [16]. RL can be utilized to maximize both cost and long-term energy efficiency. RL agents are also capable of generalizing their learned strategies to related but distinct tasks when given the right structures.

RL ALGORITHMS

Model-based and model-free algorithms are the two main categories into which RL algorithms fall, as shown in Fig. (5).

Fig. (5))
Model-based vs. model-free RL algorithm.

Algorithms can be further classified into on-policy and off-policy varieties. Within a model-based algorithm, a predefined RL model is present that gains knowledge from the initial state, actions, and state changes brought about by the actions. These kinds so save action and status data for later use. Conversely, model-free algorithms do not require state and action data to be stored in memory because they work through trial-and-error techniques. The other Algorithms are as follows:

  1. Q-learning: Q-learning is a model-free, off-policy method that picks up knowledge from arbitrary behaviour (greedy policy). In Q-learning, “Q” stands for the calibre of the tasks that optimize the gains produced by the algorithmic process. The awards that are gained are stored by the Q-learning algorithm in a reward matrix. To represent reward 50, for instance, a reward matrix is created, and a value is assigned to position 50. Methods like value iteration and policy iteration are used to update these values [17]. The term “policy iteration” describes the process of refining or improving policies by taking steps to increase their value function. The values of the value function are updated during a value iteration. The formula for Q-learning in mathematics is given by equation 1.
  2. SARSA: An on-policy technique is the State-Action-Reward-State-Action (SARSA) algorithm. As such, it deviates from the avaricious methodology of Q-learning. Rather, SARSA uses lessons from the present to implement the RL process.
  3. Deep Q-Network: or DQN, Deep Q-network employs a neural network instead of 2D arrays, in contrast to Q-learning and SARSA. When it comes to forecasting and updating the state values of typically unknown states, Q-learning methods are inefficient. Therefore, in DQN, neural networks take the place of 2D arrays in the effective computation of state values and values corresponding to state transitions, accelerating the RL learning process.
(1)

where S2 is the next state, R is the reward, beta is the discount factor, and alpha is the learning rate. Future value equals Q(S2,a).

RL TECHNIQUES

The list of different RL approaches is shown in Fig. (6):

  1. Process of Markov Decision: The goal of this algorithm is to generate a series of decisions that gradually interact with the surroundings. To help it make decisions, the agent periodically sends information about the condition of the surroundings. Following an activity, rewards are given based on the previous action, and the world changes to a new state [18]. A trajectory of behaviours, rewards, and states is produced by repeating the process. The algorithm strives to maximize overall benefits rather than simply present rewards. This is good since it steers clear of the avaricious strategy that just looks for the most benefit for the current situation. If a smaller payout can result in a larger total reward, the algorithm might choose to accept it instead.
  2. Value Iteration: This method chooses the course of action that maximizes the optimal state-value function for a particular state to determine the optimal policy, or best course of action, for that state. An iterative procedure is used to compute this ideal state-value function [19]. This method gives the algorithm its name, value iteration. The state-value function (V) is initialized using random values via the approach, and its estimate is then improved iteratively until convergence. The values of V(s) and Q(s,a) are updated at each iteration. Value iteration optimizes the state-value function until it converges to an ideal solution, ensuring the best outcomes are achieved.
Fig. (6))
Learning cycle of RL.

PRACTICAL EXAMPLES OF RL

Although the field of RL is still in its infancy, several products and applications are beginning to depend on it. Businesses are starting to use RL for challenges that call for sequential decision-making and where the technology can either automate the decision-making process or complement human specialists. The following figure gives an idea of various application fields where reinforcement is being used these days.

  1. Robotics: Since decision-making in unexpected situations is at the heart of reinforcement learning, robotics has shown a strong interest in this field. Making decisions might be easy when performing repeated and basic jobs. Empirical studies demonstrate the benefits of deep RL using deep neural networks for tasks like generalization and translating high-dimensional sensory input to controlled system outputs [20]. Research indicates that deep RL for robots is highly dependent on the gathered datasets. Therefore, current efforts are examining ways to gather real-world data and repurpose previous data to enhance RL systems.
  2. Natural Language Processing: According to recent research, using tools and techniques from natural language processing, such as Large Language Models (LLMs) may help RL systems become more general by using textual representations of real-world settings. Numerous studies demonstrate how, when training learning agents in successive decision-making tasks, interactive textual environments offer more affordable options than three-dimensional environments. The foundation of chatbots' textual decision-making is likewise deep reinforcement learning. RL really works better than other approaches to enhance chatbot dialogue response.
  3. Healthcare: Research on RL is ongoing in this field. Bots with biological intelligence have undergone rigorous training to execute precise surgical procedures. These RL bots help with disease detection, illness prognosis in the event of treatment postponement, and more.
  4. Robotics and Industries: Because robot actions are sequential, deep RL is a common use in robotics. These agents have a variety of uses in industrial automation and production because they can learn to interact with dynamic settings [21].
  5. Deep RL: It has resulted in a notable decrease in labour costs, product defects, and unplanned downtime. It also features faster changeover times and increased production speed.
  6. DeepMind's Reinforcement: These learning skills allowed Google to significantly lower the amount of energy used in its data centres. An RL technology from Bonsai, which Microsoft just bought, may be used to automate and “build intelligence into complex and dynamic systems” in the industrial, supply chain, HVAC, energy, and automotive industries.
  7. Making Predictive Maintenance Better: While ML has long been applied in the manufacturing sector, RL has the potential to improve predictive maintenance beyond its current state.
  8. Engaging in Gaming: In fact, AlphaGo, an ML program, defeated one of the top human Go players in the world, marking the first known application of RL. Nowadays, competition in a wide range of games is based on RL.
  9. Medication: Determining the best course of action for medication therapy and medical disorders is a perfect application for RL. In addition to various healthcare applications, it has also been utilized in clinical trials.
  10. Driverless Cars: Many self-driving automobiles, trucks, drones, and ships are powered by reinforcement algorithms. With the aid of reinforcement learning, Waye, a UK-based startup, created an autonomous car that could drive itself to proficiency in 20 minutes.
  11. Dealing with the issue of Energy Consumption: Thanks to the rapid advancement of AI, governments are now able to address serious issues like energy consumption. Additionally, servers have had to be alert due to the growing number of IoT devices and commercial, industrial, and corporate systems.
  12. Traffic Signal Control: As a result of urbanization and the growing demand for automobiles in big cities, traffic congestion in urban areas is becoming more and more problematic for authorities to handle. RL models offer traffic light control based on the traffic status within a locality, which addresses this problem. This suggests that the model learns, adapts, and modifies traffic light signals in urban traffic networks by considering traffic coming from various directions.
  13. Marketing: To accomplish long-term objectives, RL assists businesses in maximizing consumer growth and streamlining business methods. In the marketing domain, RL helps to make tailored suggestions to consumers by forecasting their decisions, responses, and behaviours regarding goods or services.
  14. Gaming: As RL agents continue to apply logic through their experiences and complete a series of actions to attain the intended results, they learn and adapt to the gaming environment. For the AI models available at the time, it was a huge stride. In addition to creating deep neural network games like AlphaGo, RL agents are used in game development for bug finding and game testing. Given that RL runs through several cycles without outside assistance, potential errors are simple to find. For instance, Ubisoft and other gaming firms employ RL to find flaws.

REINFORCMENT LEARNING CHALLENGES

While RL shows promise to introduce automated AI for decision-making, there are several practical obstacles that must be overcome before it can be used to solve actual industrial problems. For example, the trade-off between exploration and exploitation is one issue with reinforcement learning. Achieving equilibrium between acting on information that produces high expected benefits now (exploitation) and acting on information that may provide new knowledge and perhaps larger rewards in the future (exploration) is a challenge for the agent. Another problem is the curse of dimensionality, which asserts that since there are so many possible states and actions in a complex system, it may be difficult to develop an optimal policy. By using function approximation or other techniques to reduce the problem's dimensionality, this challenge can be solved. A few other challenges are first, academic benchmark problems predominate in the literature, and researchers are aggressively pushing the state-of-the-art in efficient algorithms on these issues. This means that you will not have many reference examples to look to for modelling inspiration or assurance that it is a workable solution for interesting industrial challenges. Secondly, a wide range of algorithms exist, such as value function-based approaches (e.g., Q learning, DQN, policy mapping-based approaches (e.g., PPO, Policy Learning), and their combinations (e.g., A2C, SAC, and actor-critic methods). It may not be feasible for many data scientists to devote the necessary time to learning about subjects like dynamic programming, Monte-Carlo techniques, Markov chains, Markov decision processes, and Bellman recursion, but doing so will help you gain a solid understanding of the inner workings of these algorithms. Moreover, several open-source frameworks, including stable-baselines, RLLib, d3rlpy, Coach, and Keras RL, have their own implementations of these different techniques. It also requires a steep learning curve to become comfortable with these implementations and know how to use them effectively, which could be restrictive for solution developers in real-world situations. Third, all these algorithms and their implementations admit multiple tunable hyperparameters and have some defaults, but as training and learning advance, RL is known to be hypersensitive to both the initial choice and the dynamic retuning of these choices. The number of frameworks, agents, and hyperparameters, the triple curse of dimensionality-requires extensive, dispersed, and computationally demanding experimentation to find the optimal algorithms and potential winning policies. Fourth, one crucial factor in RL that must be considered is sample efficiency. The learning algorithm would have very little environment exploration using training data if there were fewer data samples available in a real-world scenario. This is because the information and statements in the samples would not be completely covered.

To solve this issue, either an RL technique needs to be sample-efficient to learn the environment from a limited quantity of data, or data efficiency must be adequate. Many action and state spaces can be present in complex real-world settings, which could make using RL approaches more difficult. Several techniques have been proposed to divide these high-dimensional states among several candidates to simplify a certain job. Following safety rules is an important constraint and challenge that needs to be considered before using a certain algorithm or approach for decision-making. RL approaches need to follow safety limitations in both the operational and experimental stages to avoid a system damaging itself or its environment in real life. The above-mentioned kinds of tasks are challenging to execute since most real-world applications only have limited observability and lack complete information about the environment and situations. RL can also be hindered by system delays and agents' delayed responses in computationally demanding applications. The concepts of bias (B) and variance in RL pertain to the degree to which the reinforcement signal accurately reflects the reward function in the current environment. These concepts can have a substantial impact on the outcomes of a learning process.

USE CASES OF RL

Numerous real-world use scenarios can benefit from the application of RL:

  1. Personalization of Marketing: RL can tailor recommendations to specific users in apps such as recommendation systems, depending on their interactions. Experiences become more tailored as a result. For instance, an application might show a user advertisements depending on certain demographic data. The application learns which adverts to show the user to maximize product sales with each interaction with an advertisement.
  2. Optimization Challenges: Conventional optimization techniques assess and contrast potential solutions according to predetermined standards to find a solution. RL, on the other hand, offers the idea of learning from encounters to identify the best or nearly optimal solutions gradually. For instance, RL is used by a cloud expenditure optimization system to select the best instance kinds, numbers, and configurations while adapting to changing resource requirements. It bases its decisions on variables such as spending, use, and the state and availability of cloud infrastructure.
  3. Financial Forecasts: Financial markets have intricate dynamics with dynamic statistical characteristics. By taking transaction costs into account and responding to changes in the market, RL algorithms can maximize long-term gains. An algorithm might, for example, study the stock market's rules and tendencies before testing strategies and recording related profits. It formulates a profit-maximizing plan and dynamically generates a value function.

CONCLUSION

In summary, a third class of ML algorithms that defies classification as either supervised or unsupervised is called reinforcement learning. It all comes down to applying lessons learned from the past to optimize the gain. The model-based category, which is a greedy algorithm with the goal to maximize the reward at each step, and the model-free category, which is not a greedy algorithm and concentrates on maximizing the overall reward, but for each stage, are the two main categories of RL algorithms that have been discussed. The decision-making and learning processes are automated via reinforcement learning. It is well known that RL agents can learn from their experiences and surroundings without needing human guidance or direct supervision. Within AI and ML, RL is an essential subset. It usually helps in the development of autonomous robots, drones, or even simulators since it simulates how a human would learn to understand their environment. Ultimately, one of the most fascinating and practical aspects of ML is reinforcement learning. In virtual reality, the agent investigates its surroundings on its own, devoid of human assistance. RL has shown considerable promise in surpassing human-level performance in many complicated activities, provided a sufficiently accurate simulator exists or can be developed. It was demonstrated to be capable of learning a wide range of tasks using the same method. This might indicate considerable promise in engineering, where custom design of comparable projects incurs significant cost and time investment.

Reinforcement Learning in Practice: Real-World Applications across Industries



M.G. Harsha1, *, Amandeep Kaur1
1 School of Computer Science and Engineering, Lovely Professional University, Phagwara-144411, Punjab, India

Abstract

Reinforcement Learning (RL), a major Machine Learning (ML) paradigm, has revolutionised finance, robotics, gaming, and healthcare in the 21st century. This chapter explores the fundamentals and applications of RL. In RL, model-free and model-based techniques are defined in this chapter. Model-free methods do not require a model of the environment; instead, they rely on trial-and-error exploration. In contrast, model-based techniques include modelling the environment to make informed decisions. This distinction is crucial to understanding the subsequent discussions. Agents, environment, state, rewards, cumulative rewards, policy, and value comprise RL. Agents make decisions to maximise incentives in each situation. Agents interact with their external environment. States are environmental conditions, whereas rewards are agent feedback based on its actions. Cumulative rewards are the aggregate benefit of many actions. Policies define how agents pick actions, whereas values indicate expected total rewards for states or state-action pairs. Reinforcement learning reveals its fundamental nature through complex interactions between the agent and the environment. To optimise long-term performance, agents adapt to incentives and conditions in dynamic contexts. Additionally, this chapter describes alternative RL computational methods. Each technique, from Q-learning to Deep Q-Networks (DQN) and Proximal Policy Optimisation (PPO), has pros and downsides. In discrete action spaces, Q-learning is simple and successful, whereas DQN uses deep neural networks to handle complex state spaces in continuous domains. On the contrary, PPO is stable and efficient in policy optimisation. Real-world RL applications in numerous disciplines are discussed outside theoretical frameworks. Finance uses RL for portfolio optimisation, algorithmic trading, and risk management. Robotics uses RL for self-guided movement, manipulation, and task performance. The game uses RL to produce adaptive enemies in a bespoke game. RL methods are used by the healthcare sector to facilitate medication research, enhance treatment tactics, and customise medical treatments for patients. In summary, this chapter provides a comprehensive analysis of RL, elucidating its theoretical underpinnings, intricate algorithms, and practical implementations.

Keywords: Deep Q-Network, provide footer, Model-free, Model-based, Proximal policy optimisation, Reinforcement learning.

* Corresponding author M.G. Harsha: School of Computer Science and Engineering, Lovely Professional University, Phagwara-144411, Punjab, India; E-mail: rshamg1225@gmail.com

INTRODUCTION

RL emerged in the mid-20th century when researchers began exploring computer models of learning that drew upon principles from behaviourism and psychology. Richard Bellman pioneered the concept of “reward maximisation” in the 1950s, establishing it as one of the first influential contributions in this domain. Bellman's dynamic programming provided the theoretical foundation for solving problems related to sequential decision-making [1]. This approach laid the foundation for later progress in reinforcement learning. During the second half of the 20th century, advancements in processing power and algorithms played a significant role in driving further progress in RL. In the 1980s, Chris Watkins created Q-learning, a method for reinforcement learning that does not depend on previous knowledge of how the environment behaves. It can acquire optimal strategies. This accomplishment signified a significant milestone in the study of RL and opened possibilities for the creation of more complex applications [2]. During the 1990s, there was a significant rise in the influence of neuroscientific findings on RL algorithms. Richard Sutton, a neurologist and computer scientist, along with his collaborator Andrew Barto, introduced the concept of temporal difference learning. They forged links between RL algorithms and the learning processes seen in animals. As a result, the development of RL algorithms was inspired by biological processes, such as SARSA (State-Action-Reward-State-Action). Deep RL emerged in the early 2000s as a combination of deep learning and RL methodologies. In 2013, researchers at DeepMind Technologies unveiled the Deep Q-Network (DQN). The entity showed exceptional proficiency in acquiring the ability to play Atari games by using unaltered pixel inputs. This remarkable accomplishment generated significant interest in deep RL and laid the foundation for further study in the discipline [3]. Currently, RL remains a vibrant field with several potential applications across various sectors, such as healthcare, robotics, autonomous systems, finance, and others. The future of RL's capacity to address challenging real-world problems seems promising due to advancements in deep RL, as well as progress in algorithms, hardware, and theoretical foundations.

The primary objective of this introductory chapter is to provide readers with a comprehensive understanding of the fundamental concepts and rationales that underpin RL. The development of Artificial Intelligence (AI) has grown more imminent due to the progress in computer technology and the use of innovative intelligent algorithms [4]. AI, short for artificial intelligence, is computer software designed to simulate human brain functions and behaviour. ML is a subdivision of AI. The three major branches of ML are as follows (Fig. 1):

Fig. (1))
Types of ML.
  1. Supervised learning: Supervised learning involves training algorithms using a labelled dataset to learn from all possible input-output combinations. The objective is to determine the methodology for using a mapping function to transform input variables into corresponding output variables [5]. Both classification and regression are common tasks in machine learning. Supervised learning often employs algorithms, such as neural networks, decision trees, random forests, and Support Vector Machines (SVMs).
  2. Unsupervised Learning: Unsupervised learning is a learning approach that aims to discover patterns or structures in unlabelled data. This category of operations includes methods, such as density estimation, dimensionality reduction, and clustering. K-means clustering, hierarchical clustering, principal component analysis, and t-SNE are all approaches that belong to the category of unsupervised learning [6].
  3. Reinforcement Learning: Reinforcement learning, sometimes referred to as RL, is a branch of ML that focuses on instructing agents to make a series of choices in diverse situations to attain certain objectives. Supervised learning is a kind of learning where the model acquires knowledge by studying labelled instances. In contrast, unsupervised learning entails the model deducing patterns from unlabelled data [7]. The primary focus of RL is acquiring knowledge via interactions with an environment, distinguishing it from other forms of learning.

The purpose of this chapter is to provide readers with a comprehensive comprehension of the fundamental ideas and motivations that form the basis of RL. The chapter starts by presenting a precise description of RL as a specialised branch of ML. RL specifically emphasises training agents to make sequential decisions in dynamic environments with the aim of achieving certain goals. RL places a strong focus on the interactive aspect of learning, in which an intelligent agent gains information by actively interacting with its environment and learning from its failures. In this section, we introduce the key components of RL, including the agent, environment, states, actions, rewards, and policy. This will provide a solid foundation for comprehending the subsequent chapters. The chapter delves into the historical origins of RL, charting its inception from early computer models inspired by behaviourism to the present-day progress propelled by Deep Learning (DL) and neuroscience [8]. Furthermore, the chapter delves into the emergence of Deep RL and its substantial impact on the field. This is explained by notable developments, such as DQN, which can learn directly from unprocessed sensory input. Additionally, we investigate practical applications in several domains, such as robotics, game playing, finance, health, and recommendation systems. The first chapter concludes by laying the groundwork for further exploration of RL algorithms, applications, difficulties, and future possibilities in the subsequent chapters of the book [9]. Before delving into specifics, the distinction between RL and Supervised Learning (SL), as well as between RL and Unsupervised Learning (UL), is explained in Tables 1 and 2.

Table 1 Difference between RL and SL.
Supervised Learning Reinforcement Learning
In SL, the model learns from labelled data. In RL, the model learns to make decisions by interacting with the environment.
SL needs clear feedback in the form of marked data. The model's success is assessed by how well it can guess the correct names for data that has not been seen yet. Delay in benefits is what makes RL work. The worker gets feedback from the surroundings after acting, but it may come late or in small amounts. Through trial and error, the robot learns to connect acts with future benefits.
SL needs a collection with named instances, which are usually made up of pairs of input and output. RL does not need named data; instead, it depends on how things interact with each other in the world [10]. The agent learns by trying out different acts and seeing what happens.
SL trains the model by minimising a loss function that measures the difference between expected output and true labels. This can be done with an optimisation method like gradient descent. The models in RL learns by making mistakes. It tries out different acts and sees what happens. Then, it changes its policy based on the benefits it gets, using methods like policy gradients or Q-learning.
SL is often used for jobs that need marked data, like object recognition, classification, and regression. RL works well for problems that need to make choices and respond in a certain order, like game play, robots, and self-driving systems.

Table 2 Difference between RL and unsupervised learning.
Unsupervised Learning Reinforcement Learning
Finds patterns or structures in data without being told to, usually by grouping together similar data points or lowering the number of dimensions. By dealing with surroundings, it can learn how to make decisions in a way that maximises the total benefits.
Does not get clear input and learns from the way data is structured or distributed. Gets input later in the form of awards or punishments based on what they do in a setting.
Uses material that has not been labelled to find secret trends, correlations, or groups. It can learn by interacting with the world without needing marked data.
Usually uses optimisation techniques to lower a loss function that has to do with how data is represented or grouped. Learned by making mistakes and changing rules based on the prizes or punishments they got.
SL is often used for jobs that need marked data, like object recognition, classification, and regression. Used in situations where decisions need to be made in a certain order, like when playing robotics games or setting up an independent system [11].


LITERATURE REVIEW

In an article, Muddasar Naseem [12] discussed RL and its use in various fields. Due to advances in deep neural networks, RL has become a major tool in artificial intelligence. To optimise rewards, an agent learns the most profitable actions via trial and error. For this purpose, the agent interacts with its surroundings. The Markov Decision Process (MDP), which simulates stochastic decision-making, underpins reinforcement learning. RL is used in healthcare, robotics, communication, natural language processing, gaming, and resource management. Dynamic Programming, Monte Carlo techniques, and DQNs demonstrate reinforcement learning's ability to handle complicated problems. These methods also demonstrate RL's groundbreaking impact on several technical and industrial fields. Thanh Thi Nguyen et al. discussed that as more systems are connected to the internet, cyberattacks have increased. ML, particularly Deep RL (DRL), may help alleviate these issues. Combining DL with traditional RL, DRL can solve complex, dynamic, and high-dimensional cyber defence problems. DRL techniques for cybersecurity were reviewed in this article. It discussed DRL-based cyber-physical system security, autonomous intrusion detection, and multi-agent DRL-based game theory simulations, thus providing DRL-based cybersecurity research directions and indicating DRL's ability to solve complicated cybersecurity problems. Zhenning Li et al. noted that ineffective traffic signal regulation can lead to traffic congestion and unnecessary energy consumption. Data-driven RL is becoming increasingly popular for adaptive traffic signal management in complex urban traffic networks. Although Deep Neural Networks (DNNs) improve learning, applying deep RL to transportation networks with many signalised crossings is still difficult. Key difficulties include non-stationarity, the exploration–exploitation trade-off, multi-agent coordination, continuous action spaces, and other complex obstacles. A Multi-Agent Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (MADDPG) technique is introduced as an extension of actor-critic policy gradient approaches to address related issues. MADDPG uses centralised learning and decentralised execution. Critics utilise more knowledge to speed up training, while performers employ their local observations. Model evaluation is conducted using simulations from the Simulation of Urban Mobility (SUMO) platform. The model comparison shows that the suggested algorithm manages traffic signals well. Indu Shukla et al. examined model-free and model-based RL [13]. Model-free and model-based RL are the main methods in reinforcement learning. Both strategies attempt to solve the challenge of an agent learning to make decisions by interacting with an environment to maximise cumulative rewards. However, they vary greatly in how they absorb and utilise environmental information. Model-free or model-based RL relies on the application and sampling efficiency and computing resource utilisation trade-offs. Model-free techniques are chosen when model construction is difficult and computing resources are constrained. However, model-based methods work effectively in costly interaction situations where an appropriate model can be learned [14].

Model-free and model-based RL are combined in hybrid techniques. Dyna-Q is a hybrid technique where the agent models the environment and adjusts its policy depending on real and simulated experiences. For effective RL systems adapted to problem domains, one must grasp the pros and cons of model-free and model-based reinforcement learning. Yingfeng Cai [6] proposes a strategy to address the limitations of traditional autonomous driving control systems by incorporating Machine Learning (ML)–based methods. Conventional approaches often struggle to adapt to increasingly complex traffic scenarios. To overcome these challenges, the project introduces a multitask Reinforcement Learning (RL)–based decision control system. This innovative approach simplifies autonomous driving by breaking the process down into manageable components. This optimises training time and traffic efficiency. To ensure agent convergence efficiency and resilience, adaptive noise exploration is created [15]. This strategy dynamically adapts the exploration process to guide the agent to the best choices in changing scenarios, like an expert navigator. To thoroughly evaluate the proposed technique, the Carla simulator is used to improve autonomous vehicle driving, particularly in difficult traffic. This technology synchronises autonomous driving components to improve traffic flow, like a conductor conducting a complicated symphony. This study serves as a beacon for more sophisticated autonomous driving systems by using multi-task RL and adaptive exploration strategies to find safer, more efficient transportation options in the ever-changing mobility landscape.

Fundamental Concepts of RL

Agent

In the realm of RL, an agent is a computational entity that interacts with an environment to gain information on how to achieve a certain aim via experimenting and learning from errors, as shown in Fig. (2). The agent assesses the present state of the environment, selects actions based on its choices, and receives feedback in the form of rewards or penalties. The objective of the agent is to formulate a policy, which is a mathematical function that connects states with corresponding behaviours, with the aim of maximising the cumulative rewards acquired over a certain duration [16]. The agent's learning process involves evaluating the outcomes of its actions and adjusting its strategy to improve performance in future interactions.

Fig. (2))
Fundamentals of reinforcement learning.

Environment

In Reinforcement Learning (RL), the “world” refers to the external environment with which the agent or robot interacts. It refers to everything that is not part of the agent and is the setting in which the agent works. The agent's actions influence the environment, causing it to transition from one state to another according to predefined dynamics. In return, the world tells the agent what to do by rewarding or punishing it. There are obstacles and chances in the world that help the agent learn and change its behaviour to get the most benefits over time. The interaction between an agent and its environment is commonly represented as a Markov Decision Process (MDP) [17]. In this type of modelling, the state changes and benefits are determined by the agent's action and the current state.

State

When used in RL, a “state” is the way the world is set up or functioning at a certain time. It has all the important details that the person needs to make decisions. The agent's actions are determined by the current state, which plays a crucial role in selecting the next state and the corresponding reward. One type of state is fully observable, which means the agent knows everything about the environment; the other type is partly observable, which means the agent only has access to incomplete or inaccurate information about the environment. Depending on how complicated the world is, the state space can be classed as discrete or continuous, limited, or endless [18]. It includes all possible states.

Action

When used in RL, an action is a choice or move that the agent makes based on how the world is right now. Actions are how the person interacts with the world. The agent’s actions depend on the current state, which critically influences the transition to the next state and the associated reward. In a certain situation, the action space is made up of all the possible things a person could do. One type of action is distinct, like going left or right, and the other type is constant, like changing a vehicle's speed. Choice of action affects the agent's learning directly because it controls how the world changes and what information the agent receives. These changes are then used to change the strategy and get better at making decisions over time.

Reward

When it comes to RL, a reward is a single number signal that the world sends to the agent because of its behaviour. It defines how much the action will profit or cost right now in this state. This changes the agent's behaviour by either pushing or discouraging certain actions. The prize is a key part of learning because the agent's main goal is to get the most rewards over time. Positive incentives encourage the person to do good things, while negative incentives deter them from doing bad things. It is the reward function that decides how benefits are given to agents based on their actions and states [19]. In a big way, it changes the agent's strategy and how much they learn generally.

EXPLORATION AND EXPLOITATION

Table 3 presents the difference between exploration and exploitation.

Table 3 Difference between exploration and exploitation.
Exploration Exploitation
Gathers new information to improve future decision-making Utilizes existing knowledge to maximize immediate rewards or performances
Chooses actions that have uncertain or unknown outcomes Selects acts that are shown to provide the most rewards based on previous encounters
Involves a higher risk as it may lead to suboptimal immediate rewards Involves lower risk as it relies on well-known and tested actions
Focuses on learning and gathering more information about the environment Focuses on optimizing performance based on existing knowledge
Aims for long-term benefits and better performance through a comprehensive understanding Aims for short-term gains and maximizes current rewards
An example of an algorithm is Epsilon greedy with probability p An example of an algorithm is Epsilon greedy with probability 1-p

TYPES OF REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

The two main types of RL are model-free RL and model-based reinforcement learning. The goal of both methods is to help a robot learn how to make decisions by interacting with its surroundings in a way that maximises the total benefits it receives. They do learn and use knowledge about their surroundings in very different ways, though.

Model-Free RL

Instead of attempting to model the environment's dynamics, model-free RL methods learn solely by observing them. On the other hand, they may pick up a value or policy function just by being around the world. In any given state, the policy dictates what the agent should do, and the value function gives an approximation of the expected payout for each possible combination of states and activities. Two common types of model-free algorithms are:

  1. Q-Learning: This approach is known as an off-policy algorithm since its main goal is to determine the optimal policy for selecting actions. The Q-value for each state-action pair is updated by considering both the immediate reward received and the maximum expected future rewards. To keep the Q-values in the Q-learning algorithm up-to-date, a mathematical formula called the Q-learning update rule is used, which is as follows:

    Q(s,a)←Q(s,a)+α(r+γa′max​Q(s′,a′)-Q(s,a))

    α is the learning rate,
    γ is the discount factor,
    r is the immediate reward, and
    s1 is the next state.
  2. Policy Gradient Methods: These methods compute the gradient of expected rewards to optimize the explicitly parameterized policy parameters. When dealing with action environments that have several dimensions and continuous activity, this approach works well. A gradient rising on the expected return is used to alter the plan.

Model-Based RL

To predict the future state and reward from the current state and action, model-based RL approaches include developing a model of the dynamics of the environment. Following model comprehension, the agent may plan to use the model to simulate future interactions and fine-tune its policy accordingly. There are two main components in model-based RL:

Model Learning: This involves learning the transition dynamics P(s′/s,a) and the reward function R(s, a). Table 4 presents the difference between model-free and model-based algorithms. The model may be acquired via the use of supervised learning methodologies, whereby the agent gathers data from its interactions and constructs a model to make predictions about the outcomes.

Table 4 Difference between model-free and model-based algorithms.
Model-Free Model-Based
There is no use of any model of the environment. The agent acquires knowledge directly via its interactions with the environment. Entails acquiring and using a conceptual representation of the surroundings to facilitate the process of making choices and devising strategies.
Effective learning often necessitates increased engagement with the surrounding world. The increased sampling efficiency is attributed to the capability of simulating interactions using the acquired model.
More straightforward and easy to execute; no need to sustain an environment model. The task becomes more complex and requires more processing resources as a result of the need to acquire and use the environmental model.
Preferable in situations when constructing a precise model is challenging and there are constraints on computing resources. Thrives in situations where interactions are expensive and an appropriate model can be efficiently acquired.
Q-learning, SARSA, DQNs, and Policy Gradient approaches, such as REINFORCE. AlphaZero (combines deep learning and MCTS), Dyna-Q (combines model-free and model-based approaches), and Model-Based Policy Optimization (MBPO).

  1. Planning: Utilising the acquired model, the agent can strategize its actions by recreating the surrounding environment. Methods, such as dynamic programming, Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS), or other planning methods, may be used to determine the most effective policy or value function.
  2. Value Function: The value function is a fundamental idea in RL that allows agents to maximise their cumulative rewards over time by approximating the anticipated return, or anticipated reward, from a specific condition or state-action combination. Several RL algorithms rely on value functions. There are two primary categories of value functions:

State Value Function: The state-value function, denoted as V(S), represents the expected return when starting from the state s and following a specific policy π thereafter. Mathematically, it is defined in equation 1 as:

(1)

Where, S is the current state, and Π is the policy being followed.

Action-Value Function (Q-function): The action-value function, denoted as Q (s, a), represents the expected return when starting from states s, acting a, and subsequently following policy π. Mathematically, it is defined in equation 2 as:

(2)

Where, a is the action taken, and s is the current state.

  1. State Space: The state space is crucial in reinforcement learning because it encompasses all possible states an agent can occupy in a given environment. Every state has all the important information needed to show exactly how the world is right now. No matter how complicated the world is, the state space can be either small or infinite, and it can be classed as discrete or continuous. In the game of chess, for example, a “state” is the exact way that all the pieces are placed on the board. In a robotic guidance job, on the other hand, a state includes the robot's exact position, direction, and the data it gets from its devices. The state space is very important because it sets the rules for how the agent can make decisions. A well-defined state space makes it much easier for the robot to learn and improve its behaviour.
  2. Action Space: The set of all the possible things a person could do in any given situation is called the action space. “Action space” is the set of options or things that a person can do that will change the world right now. The action space, like the state space, can be discrete or continuous, and it can have a fixed or infinite number of options. For example, in the board game tic-tac-toe, the action space is made up of all the possible moves that can be made on the grid. When directing a robot, on the other hand, the action area might include several different motion directions or paths. What makes the learning process difficult depends on how big and how the action space is organised. This is because the agent must investigate and weigh the possible outcomes of each action to find the best plan.
  3. Rewards: In the field of RL, incentives are very important for controlling what agents do as they interact with their environment, so that they can make the most money generally. When a person completes a task, they immediately get an award for it. These awards give the agent quick input, which helps them quickly learn good habits. In a game, for example, getting points right away after getting a coin reinforces the pattern of collecting coins, leading the agent to do the same thing again and again. When agents get delayed rewards after a certain number of actions or a long time, they need to make a connection between those actions and the rewards they will get in the future. This happens a lot when doing things like figuring out a maze, where the reward of getting to the exit is not until after several steps that require smart thinking and the ability to remember what steps you have already taken. Cumulative rewards are all the rewards that a person has earned over time. Their main goal is to get the most cumulative rewards possible. This means making decisions that find a balance between short-term gains and possible long-term gains. For example, when spending, a person must choose between small gains right now and bigger returns in the future. For RL to work, the trade-offs between instant, delayed, and accumulated benefits must be well balanced. They must come up with strategies that will help them do well overall and can change based on the conditions they are in.

LEARNING METHODS IN REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

Value-Based Methods:These methods help the agent make decisions by estimating the value of different states or state-action pairs. The value function tells you how much a person is likely to gain from a certain condition or state-action combination. Finding rules that make the value function work best is a top priority for these strategies. The Q-learning method is a good example. In this method, the agent changes its opinions about the Q-values (how valuable it is to do a certain action in a certain state) by considering the benefits it gets and what it thinks the next state will be worth.

Deep Q-Network (DQN): It makes use of deep learning to successfully handle state spaces with a lot of variables, which is something that regular Q-learning algorithms would not be able to do. The DQN method uses a neural network to guess the Q-value function. This lets the robot learn effective techniques in complicated situations, even ones with visual data. The neural network is taught by using events that have been saved in a replay buffer. This helps to break the links between new experiences and makes the learning process more stable. Target networks and experience replay are two methods that DQN uses to make the learning process more stable and effective.

The Q-learning algorithm is the fundamental basis of DQN. Q-learning is an RL algorithm that seeks to find a policy that maximises the total cumulative reward. It does this by approximating the optimum action-value function Q*(s, a), which reflects the highest anticipated return that can be achieved from a given state a by performing action a and then following the optimal policy as defined in equation 3.

(3)

APPLICATION OF REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

We discussed how RL could be used in financial capital management. It is a big change from the old ways of managing financial portfolios to using RL and other more advanced computer methods. In DRL, deep neural networks are used to help RL work better in situations that are difficult and have a lot of dimensions. Modern Portfolio Theory (MPT) models are static and based on a single time. RL, on the other hand, is dynamic and based on data. It works great with a lot of different time frames, potentially even an infinite number. This shows that computers are becoming more like human traders and investment managers. RL systems work on their own and use complex algorithms or models to learn and change over time by interacting with a virtual environment that closely resembles the complex workings of real-life markets. The RL robot can use a huge amount of market data to find trends and make smart decisions due to its learning process, which is only led by data. The process of learning is ongoing and always changing. This is important for creating business plans that can work in markets that are always changing and are uncertain. This development depicts a shift from the traditional way of making financial choices based on people's emotions. RL might be able to find and take advantage of market inefficiencies just as well as humans, or even better, because it can handle large amounts of data. RL systems change the financial business in a big way because they are not affected by human psychological flaws or tiredness. They combine the information of people with the precision of computers. These days, people depend on more than just their own opinion when it comes to making financial decisions. They also depend on the accuracy and dependability of these complex computer systems. The financial portfolio management industry is undergoing a significant transformation as a result of this shift. To help people understand and make the connection between our models and the old ones, we have used a lot of robotic examples. The similarities in all the models not only give us a clear frame of reference, but they also fit very well with the main ideas and problems we see in both areas. When automatic systems are used to make trades based on set criteria, this is called algorithmic trading. RL makes these systems better by letting them learn the best ways to trade by interacting with the financial markets over time. Machines that use RL can adapt to changing market conditions, do jobs more efficiently, and balance the risks and benefits of different options.

CONCLUSION

This chapter has presented a fundamental comprehension of RL, clarifying its fundamental concepts, essential elements, and many applications. Through differentiating between model-free and model-based methods and examining the progression from early behaviourist models to advanced deep learning techniques, we have emphasised the ability of RL to make dynamic decisions in intricate situations. The practical applications of RL in domains like as finance highlight its potential to transform companies by employing adaptive techniques and maximising performance. This paves the way for a more thorough examination of the methods, difficulties, and prospects of RL in the next chapters.
