Evolution of Reinforcement Learning in Various Applications: Recent Trends



Ravinder Singh1, *, Krishan Dutt1, Mathias Agbeko2
1 School of Computer Application, Lovely Professional University, Punjab, India
2 Department of ICT Education, University of Education, South Campus, Winneba, Ghana

Abstract

Recent years have seen incredible progress in Reinforcement Learning (RL), which is pointing the field in the direction of bright prospects. This chapter examines the new developments, possible paths, and directions in the field of RL. The field of RL is changing quickly, from the incorporation of deep learning architectures to the introduction of meta-learning strategies. Furthermore, new avenues for the use of RL in a variety of disciplines are revealed by investigating multi-agent systems and transfer learning paradigms. The chapter also provides insight into the emerging neuroevolutionary area and how it works in tandem with RL algorithms. To guarantee the proper deployment and development of RL systems, ethical aspects and social implications are also investigated. This chapter acts as a compass for academics and practitioners, helping them fully realise the promise of RL in solving challenging real-world problems by outlining these trends and opportunities.

Keywords: Deep learning, RL algorithms, RL systems, Sample efficiency, Transfer learning.

* Corresponding author Ravinder Singh: School of Computer Application, Lovely Professional University, Punjab, India; E-mail: aarkaybca@gmail.com

INTRODUCTION

RL has attracted a lot of interest lately because of its amazing capacity to teach machines to learn and make judgements in intricate, dynamic contexts. This abstract investigates new developments, prospects, and paths in the field of RL. Initially, we explore the development of Deep RL (DRL), which makes use of RL algorithms in conjunction with deep learning approaches to facilitate more effective learning and decision-making. DRL brings up new possibilities for tackling real-world challenges across several domains when combined with other state-of-the-art technologies like computer vision and natural language processing [1].

The use of RL individual or group objectives is another exciting development. Moreover, recent advancements in meta-RL have demonstrated potential for helping agents adapt and learn effectively in a variety of settings and activities. Meta-learning approaches help RL systems generalise more effectively and use

less training data, which speeds up the implementation of RL solutions in real-world scenarios. This chapter, titled “Evolution of Reinforcement Learning in Various Applications: Recent Trends,” is a lighthouse that illuminates the way forward in this dynamic discipline as academics explore the intricacies of this area and push the frontiers of what is possible.

We provide insights into RL in this chapter, showing how state-of-the-art research, emerging technologies, and practical applications all come together. We explore the most recent developments that are reshaping the field of RL, looking at how cutting-edge computational structures, theoretical discoveries, and innovative algorithms are changing the fundamental principles of machine learning and interaction. Moreover, we face the potential and difficulties that come with deploying RL agents in intricate, dynamic ecosystems as RL continues to move from controlled laboratory settings to real-world circumstances. Applications for RL are numerous, offering a wide range of opportunities and moral dilemmas. These include autonomous robots, healthcare, and finance.

Overview of RL

In the field of machine learning known as RL, an agent gains decision-making skills by acting in a way that maximises the sum of its rewards. RL is more akin to the trial-and-error learning process observed in people and animals, as it involves learning through interaction, as opposed to supervised learning, where the model learns from a collection of labeled data [2].

Among the Fundamental Concepts in RL are Agent

The pupil or choice-maker. In RL, the agent is the core entity that interacts with the environment to learn how to achieve specific goals.

Roles of an Agent

  • Observation: The agent senses the condition of the surroundings. This state may be partially observable, in which case the agent may only access a restricted amount of pertinent data, or completely observable, in which case the agent has access to all pertinent data.
  • Action Selection: The agent chooses an action in accordance with its policy based on the observed situation.
  • Learning: Based on incentives obtained and transitions witnessed, the agent modifies its policy or knowledge. This entails modifying the policy in order to enhance performance in the future.
  • Exploration and Exploitation: The agent needs to strike a balance between exploitation (selecting activities that are known to produce large rewards) and exploration (trying novel behaviours to learn their effects).

Environment:It is the outside system that the agent communicates with. In RL (RL), the environment is the external system with which the agent interacts.

  • State (s): It is an illustration of the environment's current state or configuration. The agent's decision-making context is provided by the state.
  • Action (a): It is the agent's range of options for actions or choices. The action space might be continuous (such as changing a robot arm's speed or angle) or discrete (such as moving left, right, up, or down) [1].
  • Reward (r): It is the scalar feedback signal that the agent gets in response to an action.
  • Transition Dynamics (P): It is a set of laws or odds that dictate how the environment changes when an agent acts in it.

Types of Environments

  • Stochastic and Deterministic Environments: There is no element of chance; instead, the subsequent state and reward are entirely decided by the previous state and behaviour. Action consequences are unclear due to the probability of the next state and reward.
  • Continuous and Episodic Environments: The exchange is broken up into discrete episodes, each of which has a distinct start and finish point. The agent seeks to maximise the discounted cumulative reward or long-term average reward during the infinitely extended interaction [3].
  • Dynamic and Static Environments: Over time, the dynamics of the environment remain constant. Further, the environment may change without the agent's intervention, possibly due to other agents or outside influence.

Environment-related Applications in RL

  • Video games: Rich and intricate environments offer opportunities for RL agents to pick up techniques and methods.
  • Robotics: Robots learn to navigate, manipulate, and assemble in environments that mimic real-world areas [4].
  • Healthcare: By simulating patient reactions to medicines, RL agents can create individualised treatment regimens.
  • Autonomous Driving: Agents are trained to navigate and make safe driving judgements in environments that mimic real-world driving circumstances.

Action Types in RL

There are two types of action in RL:

  • Discrete Actions: There is a finite or countable action space A. These actions are typical in settings, such as board games or grid worlds, where movements (e.g., up, down, left, right) might represent actions.
  • Continuous Actions: Since the action space AA is continuous, the agent must select one from an endless pool of options. This is common in control jobs where actions might be exact motor commands, such as autonomous driving or robotic arm movement.

Differences between Stochastic and Deterministic Actions: Stochastic policies define a probability distribution over actions (a|s)π(a|s), from which an action is sampled to allow for exploration and variability in behaviour. Deterministic policies map a state to a specific action a=(s)a=π(s). The action is chosen deterministically based on the current state.

Strategies for Action Selection

  • Exploration and Exploitation: Finding a balance between exploitation (selecting acts that are known to give big rewards) and exploration (trying new actions to learn their effects) is one of the main issues in role-playing games.
  • Exploration: Exploration involves choosing haphazard or less definite acts to learn more about the surroundings.

Rewards in RL

  • Learning Objective: The main purpose of an RL agent is to learn a policy, or action selection strategy, that maximises the predicted cumulative reward, also known as the return.
  • Mechanism for Feedback: Reward systems offer insight into how well activities are working. While negative rewards deter poor behaviour, positive rewards promote positive behaviour.
  • Policy Optimisation: The agent employs a variety of algorithms, including Q-learning, policy gradients, and deep RL methods like Proximal Policy Optimisation (PPO) and DQN, to change its policy based on the reward signals [5].

Representation of Policies

Tabular Representation: The policy is represented as a table with each entry corresponding to a state-action pair. This representation is appropriate for situations with a small, discrete state space.

Function Approximation: Neural networks are typically used for their implementation (as in DRL). They are utilised in contexts with vast or continuous state spaces.

A function approximator, such as a neural network, is used to represent the policy. It receives the state as an input and produces actions or action probabilities.

Key Concepts

  • Policy Gradient: This technique aims to find the gradients of the expected return with respect to the policy parameters to optimize the policy. To optimise the expected return, the policy is changed in the gradient's direction [6].
  • Methods of Actor Criticism: These methods integrate approaches based on policy and value functions. Examples include the Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DDPG) and the Advantage Actor-Critic (A2C) algorithms. In these methods, the critic evaluates the policy by estimating value functions, while the actor updates or improves the policy.

HISTORY OF RL

The goal of developing intelligent systems that can learn from and adapt to complicated surroundings has motivated multidisciplinary collaboration and constant innovation throughout RL's history [7]. With its origins in behaviourism, RL was initially proposed as a theory to account for how animals learn. Psychology and neuroscience have a close relationship with RL. The contributions of Alan Turing, Norbert Wiener, and Richard Bellman to the early development of RL are also included. We have covered the extensive background and groundbreaking developments in the arena of RL in the following section:

Early Origins (1950s–1960s)

  • Behaviourist psychology, notably the work of B.F. Skinner, who investigated how animals learn through positive and negative reinforcement, is credited with giving rise to the concept of operant conditioning, which is closely related to the idea of RL.
  • Richard Bellman established dynamic programming as a technique for resolving optimisation issues in the 1950s, which marked the beginning of early computational work on RL. RL algorithms have their theoretical roots in Bellman's work.

Emergence of Temporal Difference Learning in the 1980s

  • Richard Sutton first proposed the idea of Temporal Difference (TD) learning in the 1980s. It is a cornerstone of RL. Value function updates by bootstrapping from successive estimations are the foundation of TD learning algorithms.
  • The creation of TD-Gammon, a backgammon programme that showcased the efficacy of RL in intricate tasks, was facilitated by Sutton's efforts, especially in collaboration with his colleague Andrew Barto [8].

Q-Learning (1990s)

  • Chris Watkins devised the model-free RL algorithm known as Q-learning in 1989. Q-learning relies on quantifying the quality of actions (Q-values) and iteratively updating them with the Bellman equation.
  • Q-learning emerged as a key algorithm in RL, offering a straightforward yet efficient method for discovering the best policies in Markov decision processes.

In-depth RL in the 2010s

  • The incorporation of deep neural networks into RL algorithms gave rise to deep RL, or DRL. As a result, RL algorithms can handle difficult tasks and high-dimensional state spaces.
  • In 2013, Deep Q-Networks (DQN) were introduced by researchers at DeepMind, marking one of the major advancements in DRL. DQN successfully learned control rules from unprocessed sensory input by fusing Q-learning with deep neural networks.
  • The triumph of DQN paved the way for numerous advancements in DRL, including algorithms such as Proximal Policy Optimisation (PPO), Trust Region Policy Optimisation (TRPO), and Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient (DDPG).

Current Era (2010s–Present)

  • RL has continued to progress rapidly thanks to advancements, such as model-based RL, meta-learning, and hierarchical RL.
  • Researchers have also explored the application of RL in various fields, including robotics, gaming, finance, healthcare, and autonomous driving.
  • The adaptability of big language models beyond natural language processing has been demonstrated by OpenAI's GPT (Generative Pre-trained Transformer) models, especially GPT-3, which have also shown promise in RL tasks.

OBJECTIVES AND SCOPE

The goal of ” Evolution of Reinforcement Learning in Various Applications: Recent Trends” is to offer a thorough and insightful examination of the development and status of RL. The objective of this chapter is to map the evolution of RL over time, explaining significant turning points and underlying ideas that have influenced the discipline [9]. It also seeks to recognise and evaluate current RL research trends, emphasising new developments and breakthroughs. The following are the objectives of the chapter:

Evolution and Historical Context

  • Present a thorough account of RL’s beginnings and development.
  • Explore significant turning points and discoveries in the evolution of RL methods and algorithms.

Basic Concepts

  • Describe the fundamental ideas and elements of RL, such as environments, rewards, policies, and value functions, as well as agents [10].
  • Explain different RL algorithms, including Actor-Critic, Policy Gradient, Q-learning, and DQN.

Spectrum of Applications

  • Provide examples of the numerous applications in which RL has been successfully used.
  • Emphasise cases from several fields, including autonomous systems, robotics, gaming, finance, and healthcare.

Research and Application Trends in RL

  • Identify and discuss the most recent developments in scalability, computing efficiency, and methods in RL research.
  • Examine the ways in which RL is combined with computer vision, neural networks, and deep learning [11].

FUNDAMENTALS OF RL

A subfield of machine learning called RL studies how agents should behave in each environment to maximise cumulative reward. The agent's task is to discover a strategy that maximises the cumulative reward over time by mapping states to actions. In RL, the question of exploitation vs exploration is crucial. Exploitation employs well-known acts that have a high rate of return, whereas exploration tries new things to see how they work [12]. Robotics, autonomous driving, gaming, finance, healthcare, and other fields have all seen successful applications of RL, demonstrating the technology's adaptability and ability to address a wide range of sequential decision-making challenges.

Algorithms in RL

Numerous algorithms are available in the field of RL, each specifically designed to handle a distinct facet of learning and making decisions in unpredictable contexts. Here is a thorough explanation of some of the most significant Reinforcement Learning (RL) algorithms, including the following key methods:

DRL Algorithms

To solve problems with high-dimensional state and action spaces, DRL blends deep learning methods with the concepts of RL [13]. An extensive summary of the key deep RL methods is provided below:

The Gradient of Deep Deterministic Policy:This is an off-policy, actor-critic algorithm. By applying a deterministic policy gradient technique, DQN is extended to continuous action spaces. DDPG combines the advantages of policy gradients with Q-learning, making it well-suited for systems with continuous action spaces [14].

Actor-critic, or Asynchronous Advantage Actor-Critic

To increase learning stability and efficiency, the environment is run in parallel across many instances, each with its own copy of the agent. This use of parallelism reduces training time and decreases variation.

ADVANCEMENTS IN RL TECHNIQUES

Over the past few decades, RL has made significant strides, moving from theoretical concepts to real-world, highly effective implementations. The advancement of algorithms, computing capacity, and application areas has propelled the development of RL approaches. This chapter examines these developments, emphasising significant patterns and potential paths for RL [15].

Training agents in complex environments like video games and robotics is now possible thanks to the revolutionary way that deep learning and RL are integrated, as demonstrated by DQN and Actor-Critic methods like A3C and SAC.

Deep RL

Key Innovations

  • Deep Q-Networks (DQN): This technique combines deep neural networks and Q-learning to enable RL to process high-dimensional input fields, such as unprocessed video game images.
  • Policy Gradient Methods: By directly optimizing policies, algorithms such as Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO) and REINFORCE make it possible to learn effectively in continuous action spaces.
  • Actor-Critic Methods: By balancing the advantages of policy-based and value-based approaches, algorithms, such as A3C (Asynchronous Advantage Actor-Critic) and SAC (Soft Actor-Critic) promote more reliable and effective learning [16].

MODEL-BASED REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

The goal of Model-based RL (MBRL) is to enhance learning efficiency and decision-making by enabling the agent to create a model of its surrounding environment. When it comes to predicting future states and rewards, MBRL openly builds and employs a model, in contrast to model-free approaches that learn policies directly from experience without using an explicit model of the environment.

Principal Developments

  • World Models: Methods, such as Model Predictive Control (MPC), employ acquired environmental models to schedule activities, enhancing effectiveness and output.
  • AlphaGo and AlphaZero: These ground-breaking algorithms attain superhuman performance in challenging games by combining deep learning, model-based techniques, and Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS).

Advantages of Model-Based Reinforcement Learning

  • Sample Efficiency:

MBRL frequently outperforms model-free techniques in terms of sample efficiency when it employs a model. With the model, the agent may simulate a wide range of future scenarios without requiring extra interactions with the real world.

• Improved Generalisation:

By using the model to anticipate and adjust to new circumstances, MBRL can make more accurate generalizations from a limited amount of data.

• Better Planning:

An accurate model enables the agent to plan and make informed decisions, ultimately improving performance over the long term.

Obstacles in Model-Based Reinforcement Learning

  • Model Correctness: The correctness of the learned model has a significant impact on MBRL performance. Inaccurate models may result in ineffective performance and bad policy choices.
  • Computational Complexity: Creating and using models may be computationally costly, particularly in contexts of complicated dynamics or high-dimensional landscapes.
  • Model and Policy Learning in Balance: It is critical to strike the correct balance between policy learning and model learning. Insufficient model accuracy can result in subpar policy performance, while an excessive emphasis on model learning might impede policy progress.

Prominent Instances and Utilisations

  • AlphaGo and AlphaZero: These algorithms create models for playing challenging board games like Go and Chess by combining deep learning and MCTS. They utilize trained models to simulate future game conditions, enabling them to plan their movements effectively.
  • Robotics: Accurate models of a robot's dynamics can significantly enhance learning efficiency and performance. MBRL is frequently utilized in robotics for tasks, such as navigation and manipulation.
  • Autonomous Driving: Safe and effective driving techniques are planned using models of traffic patterns and vehicle dynamics.

Within the RL paradigm, model-based RL is a powerful technique that offers enhanced planning skills, increased sample efficiency, and the potential for improved generalization [17]. Despite its challenges, research continues to be conducted to enhance MBRL approaches, which may lead to significant breakthroughs and broader applicability across various sectors.

MULTI-AGENT REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

In a subfield of RL known as Multi-Agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL), multiple agents interact and learn simultaneously in a shared environment. To accomplish its own objectives, which may entail collaboration, rivalry, or a combination of the two, each agent aims to maximize its own policy. The dynamic interactions between agents, where the decisions and learning processes of one agent can have a direct impact on the outcomes and processes of other agents, are what give rise to the complexity of MARL.

Key Innovations in Multi-Agent RL (MARL)

  • Competitive and Cooperative Situations: MADDPG (Multi-Agent Deep Deterministic Policy Gradient) and other algorithms enable agents to learn in both competitive and cooperative situations.
  • Decentralized Learning: Agents can learn and act autonomously while demonstrating coordinated behavior through methods that enable decentralized training and execution.
  • Cooperative Interaction Types: In cooperative Multi-agent Reinforcement Learning (MARL), agents work together to achieve a shared objective. They aim to maximize the group’s overall performance while distributing the benefits among themselves. Examples include collaborative tasks, sensor networks, and coordinated robots.
  • Rivalry: Agents in competitive MARL compete against one another, and one agent's success frequently indicates another's failure. Adversarial situations and games, such as chess, are two examples.
  • Indifference: Agents may need to collaborate in certain circumstances while competing in others, as is often the case in many real-world settings that involve a combination of cooperation and competition. Autonomous driving in traffic and trading on the open market are two examples.

REINFORCEMENT LEARNING IN ROBOTICS

Reinforcement learning in robotics is a dynamic and evolving discipline that combines robotic control systems and artificial intelligence, enabling robots to learn from and adapt to their surroundings through trial and error.

In robotics, RL is a powerful technique that enables robots to interact with their surroundings, learn new behaviors, and enhance existing ones autonomously.

  • Autonomous Navigation: Robots using autonomous navigation can learn the best routes and manoeuvres to navigate across intricate and changing surroundings due to RL.
  • Manipulation and Control: Robotics uses RL to accomplish tasks that require precise movement and flexibility. This encompasses activities, such as moving and arranging items, assembling pieces, and utilizing various tools and gadgets [18].

REINFORCEMENT LEARNING IN HEALTHCARE

The use of RL in healthcare is a rapidly expanding topic that leverages the capacity of RL algorithms for learning and adaptation to improve patient outcomes and streamline administrative procedures. Treatment planning, predictive analytics, personalized medicine, and diagnostics will all be significantly impacted by this strategy, which will transform the way healthcare is provided and elevate overall standards of care [19].

  • Personalized Medicine: The goal of personalized medicine is to tailor medical care to a patient's unique genetics, lifestyle, and environment.
  • Treatment Planning: RL algorithms assist in the creation and optimization of treatment regimens during treatment planning to maximize patient outcomes.
  • Diagnostics and Predictive Analytics: RL can have a significant impact in two key areas: predictive analytics and diagnostics. By analyzing large volumes of patient data, RL algorithms can identify patterns and predict the progression of diseases, helping physicians make more informed decisions.

REINFORCEMENT LEARNING IN FINANCE

In the financial sector, RL is transforming the landscape with its potent tools for algorithmic trading, portfolio management, risk assessment, and fraud detection. Financial organisations can improve their investment strategies, reduce risk, and make better decisions by utilising RL algorithms [20].

  • Trading Algorithms: Algorithmic trading involves using automated systems to execute a large volume of trades quickly based on predetermined criteria.
  • Portfolio Management: In portfolio management, RL plays a key role in constructing and adjusting investment portfolios to meet specific financial goals while managing risks.
  • Fraud Detection and Risk Assessment: Financial organizations use RL algorithms to more effectively identify and mitigate potential risks and fraudulent activities.

APPLICATIONS IN THE REAL WORLD AND CASE STUDIES

Numerous case studies and real-world applications demonstrate the success of RL in the financial industry. For instance, RL algorithms are utilized by hedge funds and private trading companies to develop high-frequency trading techniques that capitalize on transient market inefficiencies.

RL in Gaming: The gaming industry has been significantly impacted by RL, which has revolutionized game AI and development, improved user experience and balance, and produced incredible success stories with groundbreaking initiatives like AlphaGo and OpenAI Five [21].

  • AI and Development for Games: To generate smart, adaptable opponents and Non-Player Characters (NPCs) that create interesting and challenging gameplay, RL is a popular technique in game AI.
  • Balance and Player Experience: RL plays a vital role in maintaining game balance and enhancing user experiences by dynamically adjusting challenges, rewards, and other elements of gameplay.
  • Success Stories (AlphaGo, OpenAI Five): The real-life gaming success stories of AlphaGo and OpenAI Five are noteworthy. In 2016, DeepMind's AlphaGo demonstrated the strength of RL when it defeated world-class Go players, including Lee Sedol.
  • Applications in the Real World and Case Studies: Beyond these well-known projects, RL is being increasingly utilized in all facets of game design and development.

REINFORCEMENT LEARNING IN AUTONOMOUS SYSTEMS

To enable self-driving cars, drones, and unmanned vehicles to navigate complex surroundings, make real-time decisions, and ensure safety, RL is essential to the advancement of autonomous systems.

  • Autonomous Vehicles: RL algorithms are employed in the field of self-driving automobiles to teach the vehicles how to traverse roadways, read traffic signals, and safely engage with pedestrians and other vehicles.
  • Unmanned Aerial Vehicles (UAVs): RL algorithms enable drones and unmanned vehicles to perform a variety of tasks, including package delivery, airborne surveillance, search and rescue operations, and environmental monitoring.
  • Ethics and Safety: Although RL has great promise for advancing autonomous systems, safety and ethical concerns must take precedence.

REINFORCEMENT LEARNING IN NATURAL LANGUAGE PROCE-SSING

In the field of machine learning known as RL, an agent gains decision-making skills by acting in a way that maximizes cumulative reward in its environment. RL has been shown to be a useful tool in Natural Language Processing (NLP), complementing conventional approaches with its dynamic approach to learning from feedback and interactions.

Conversational Frameworks: The goal of dialogue systems, also referred to as chatbots or conversational agents [22], is to simulate human-user dialogue. In this field, RL is very helpful for several reasons:

  • Dynamic Interaction: To achieve specific objectives, dialogue systems must handle multi-turn discussions and adjust their responses to user input (e.g., purchasing a ticket, answering questions).
  • Reward Structure: In a dialogue system, the reward may be created according to several factors, including task completion rate, user satisfaction, and length of conversation.
  • Learning Policy: RL aids in the acquisition of the best policies for producing answers.

Interpretation of Languages: Translating text from one language to another is called language translation [23]. Although there has been progress with classical approaches, such as Transformer topologies and sequence-to-sequence models (Seq2Seq), RL can improve translation quality even further by concentrating on long-term sequence optimisation.

  • Rewarding Coherence and Accuracy: Throughout lengthier text sequences, translation professionals can be rewarded for preserving context, grammatical accuracy, and semantic accuracy [24].
  • Exposure Bias: Conventional models create sequences token by token during inference, and because they are trained on ground-truth data, exposure bias arises.
  • Enhancing Unusual Translations: Since unusual words and phrases are frequently underrepresented in training data, RL may be used to especially enhance translations by rewarding accurate handling of these circumstances.

Determining the Sentiment

Determining whether a sentiment is expressed as good, negative, or neutral in a text is known as sentiment analysis. By helping models comprehend context more fully and adjust their predictions in response to feedback, RL can improve sentiment analysis.

  • Contextual Understanding: Sentiment may be greatly influenced by context. RL aids in the learning of policies that take long-term dependencies in text into account, enhancing the comprehension of complex feelings.
  • Dynamic Reward Signals: In sentiment analysis, rewards may be determined by user input, sentiment prediction accuracy, or even the impact of the forecast on subsequent activities, such as customer service replies [25].
  • Adaptive Learning: Recursive Learning (RL) enables sentiment analysis models to continually adjust and enhance in response to fresh information and evolving linguistic patterns.

ENERGY MANAGEMENT WITH RL

In the field of energy management, RL monitors the performance of diverse energy systems. This section examines the application of RL in three key domains: resource allocation, smart grids, and sustainable solutions.

Intelligent Grids: Smart grids are sophisticated electricity systems that recognise and respond to changes in local consumption patterns using digital communication technologies. A key component of smart grid operation optimisation is RL.

  • Demand Response: By determining the optimal plans for load shedding and shifting, RL algorithms can effectively handle demand response. This lowers peak loads, balances supply and demand, and saves energy expenses.
  • Energy Storage Management: Energy storage devices, such as batteries, are often integral to smart grids.
  • Distributed Energy Resources (DERs): Solar panels and wind turbines are examples of DERs that RL assists in integrating and managing. RL improves the reliability and efficiency of energy distribution through the prediction of generation patterns and subsequent adjustments to grid operations.

CHALLENGES AND LIMITATIONS OF RL

Although RL has shown great promise in several sectors, several issues and restrictions still need to be resolved before it can be used more extensively and practically. Computational complexity, sample efficiency, and problems with generalisation and transfer learning are important for consideration.

Complexity of Computation

  • High Computational Requirements: RL techniques, particularly DRL models, demand a significant amount of computing power.
  • Scalability Issues: The amount of computing power needed grows exponentially with the complexity of the environment or the number of activities. This makes applying RL solutions to complex, real-world issues very difficult.
  • Hardware Restrictions: The high-performance computing infrastructure required to train complex RL models is not available to all institutions or researchers. This restricts the applicability and practical implementation of RL in several scenarios.

It can be difficult to strike an effective balance between exploration and exploitation, which involves the practice of trying new things to see how they affect outcomes and applying existing activities to maximize benefits.

  • Data Efficiency: In many real-world situations, it may not be possible to collect sufficient data to train RL models.
  • Sample Efficiency: In the context of RL, sample efficiency refers to an algorithm's ability to discover efficient policies from a relatively small number of interactions with the environment.
  • Algorithm Design: The sampling efficiency of various RL algorithms varies. For instance, since they may use the learnt model to produce more training data, model-based RL algorithms—which aim to create a model of the environment—are frequently more sample-efficient than model-free algorithms.
  • Exploration Strategy: By ensuring that the agent collects a diverse range of insightful experiences, effective exploration methods can significantly enhance sample efficiency. In this context, techniques, such as intrinsic motivation or curiosity-driven inquiry might be helpful.
  • Representation Learning: With more efficient representation learning, the agent may make better generalisations with fewer examples. Sample efficiency can be enhanced through methods such as feature extraction, state abstraction, or representation learning using neural networks.
  • Off-Policy Learning: Because off-policy algorithms (like Q-learning or Deep Q-Networks (DQN) may learn from data generated by a different policy, they can be more sample-efficient (e.g., employing experience replay buffers to reuse earlier experiences).
  • Transfer Learning: Increasing sample efficiency may also be achieved by utilising existing knowledge from similar activities, or transfer learning. An agent can pick up new jobs faster if it already knows how to do related activities.
  • Human Demonstrations: This method, also known as imitation learning or learning from demonstrations, can bootstrap the learning process and increase sampling efficiency by using demonstrations from people or expert systems.

Research on increasing sample efficiency is ongoing in RL with the goal of making these algorithms more useful and adaptable to a wider variety of real-world issues.

Transfer Learning and Generalization: Transfer learning is the process of using information from one task (or group of tasks) to enhance learning on a related but dissimilar task. Reusing previously learned abilities, models, or experiences can help to decrease training time and increase sampling efficiency. The following are important elements and techniques of transfer learning in RL:

  • Feature Transfer: Feature transfer is the process of leveraging previously learnt characteristics to express states or actions in a different activity. This might entail using other learned representations or neural network layers that have already been trained.
  • Task Similarity: Task similarity is the ability to recognise activities that have similar dynamics, structures, or regulations.
  • Policy Transfer: Transferring a policy from one setting to another is known as policy transfer. Calculating the expected return from each state, from one job to another, is known as value function transfer.
  • Multi-task Learning: Multi-task learning is the process of training a single model on multiple tasks simultaneously, enabling it to develop generic representations applicable to all tasks.
  • Curriculum Learning: Curriculum learning is the process of creating a series of activities that become increasingly challenging over time, enabling the agent to draw on previously acquired information to tackle more complex tasks.

TRENDS IN RL RESEARCH

Interdisciplinary techniques are becoming increasingly beneficial to RL research, as they draw on multiple scientific and technical fields to enhance its methodology, applications, and efficiency. The following section provides thorough descriptions of the many disciplines that contribute to RL:

  • Biological Inspiration: Using knowledge about how the human brain processes information and makes decisions to create RL algorithms that are more effective.
  • Neuro-symbolic RL: Merging neural network-based and symbolic reasoning techniques to capitalise on their respective advantages.

Psychology

  • Behavioural Cloning: Using psychology theories on learning and behaviour, and training RL bots on human behavioural data.
  • Human-in-the-Loop: Increasing learning efficiency and bringing agent behaviours into line with human expectations by incorporating human input straight into the RL training process.

Economics

  • Game Theory: Using game theory concepts in multi-agent RL settings where agents must learn how to cooperate or compete.
  • Incentive Design: Creating incentive structures that compel agents to act in a way that leads to the intended results by using economic theory.

Theory of Control

  • Optimal Control: Applying control theory methods to create RL algorithms that are more adept at managing dynamic and unpredictable contexts.
  • Robust Control: Ensuring RL agents continue to function and remain stable despite environmental changes or inaccurate models by applying robust control.

COMBINING AI WITH OTHER DOMAINS

The integration of AI with other domains includes the following:

Vision on Computers

  • Perception and Action: Integrating RL with computer vision to allow agents to comprehend and engage with intricate visual environments, as those seen in robotics or autonomous vehicles.
  • End-to-End Learning: Enhancing performance in visually demanding tasks by teaching agents to carry out tasks straight from raw sensory input.

Automation

  • Sim-to-Real Transfer: Creating methods to bridge the reality gap by transferring knowledge from virtual settings to actual robotic applications.
  • Multi-Modal Learning: Using sensory input from several sources (such as vision, touch, and sound) to enhance robotic perception and control is known as multi-modal learning.

Cognizant Computing

  • Hybrid Systems: Combining RL and symbolic AI to produce systems with enhanced reasoning, learning, and adaptability.
  • Explainability: Improving RL models' interpretability by using cognitive models that shed light on how decisions are made.

CLOUD COMPUTING AND BIG DATA'S PLACE IN RL

Large Data

  • Data-driven approaches: Using sizable datasets to pre-train RL models to enhance performance and minimize the amount of necessary online learning.
  • Data augmentation: Producing a variety of training data to enhance RL agents' capacity for generalisation.

Cloud Processing

  • Scalability: Increasing RL training through the utilisation of cloud computing capacity to allow for the deployment of more sophisticated models and wider settings.
  • Distributed RL: Putting in place distributed RL algorithms that can parallelize the learning process over several computers, greatly accelerating training times.

Working Together and Sharing

  • Platforms and Tools: Sharing RL models, datasets, and environments via cloud-based platforms to foster cooperation and accelerate research.
  • Benchmarking: Regularly assessing and contrasting RL algorithms through the use of common benchmarks and cloud-hosted, uniform settings.

FUTURE DIRECTIONS IN RL

Future developments in RL have the potential to revolutionise the discipline in several exciting ways. Creating scalable and effective algorithms that can learn from fewer interactions with the environment is a primary goal, as it will increase sample efficiency. In this context, methods, such as transfer learning, meta-learning, and model-based RL are anticipated to be crucial. Furthermore, improving the robustness and generalisation of RL strategies to manage uncertainty and perform well on a variety of tasks is essential for real-world applications. Natural and efficient learning processes can result from incorporating ideas from multidisciplinary disciplines, including neuroscience, psychology, and economics.

Effective and Scalable Algorithms

  • Enhanced Sample Efficiency: The volume of data needed to train efficient models is one of the main issues facing RL. The goal of future work is to develop algorithms that can learn from fewer examples with greater efficiency.
  • Scalable Learning: Scalability becomes important when applying RL to problems that are more complicated.

Broad Application and Sturdiness

  • Generalizable Policies: One of the main objectives is to create policies that function well in a range of situations and settings.
  • Robustness to Uncertainty: For real-world applications, it is crucial that RL agents can withstand adversarial situations and uncertainty.

Combining Other AI Frameworks

  • Hybrid Systems: Hybrid systems combine RL with other AI paradigms, such as symbolic AI, to leverage the advantages of each.
  • Multidisciplinary Approaches: RL techniques can be further enhanced by integrating knowledge from various fields, including neuroscience, psychology, economics, and control theory.

Self-governing Agents

  • Lifelong Learning: Creating agents with the ability to continually learn from ongoing encounters without needing to restart or reset is known as “lifelong learning.”
  • Self-Supervised Learning: This approach can reduce reliance on explicit reward signals and increase the flexibility and scalability of learning by having agents learn from unlabeled data by producing their own supervisory signals.

Implications for Ethics and Society: Fairness and Bias

  • Mitigating Bias: It is crucial to ensure that RL algorithms do not reinforce or magnify biases already present in the training set.
  • Making Just Decisions: A major priority is developing regulations that ensure fair outcomes for all users, particularly in sensitive areas, such as recruitment, criminal justice, and healthcare.

Responsibility and Openness

  • Explainability: People should be enabled to comprehend and examine the decision-making processes of RL models by making them comprehensible.
  • Ethical rules and Standards: Prior to deploying RL systems, ethical rules and standards must be established.

Dependability and Safety

  • Safe Exploration: Creating RL algorithms capable of exploration and learning without harming people or incurring unacceptably high risks is known as “safe exploration.”
  • Strengths and Failures:RL systems should be capable of resolving unforeseen circumstances amicably and without experiencing catastrophic failures

Impact on Society

  • Job Displacement:It should be examined how automation and RL may affect employment. This entails creating plans to mitigate job loss and facilitate workforce transitions, such as retraining initiatives and assistance measures for displaced workers.
  • Privacy Issues:RL systems should respect user privacy, particularly when using personal contexts or learning from user data. Techniques for protecting privacy and adhering to regulations are important areas of attention.

CONCLUSION

In this chapter, “Evolution of Reinforcement Learning in Various Applications: Recent Trends,” we have emphasised the revolutionary influence and continuous progress in RL. Important developments in RL algorithms include improvements in sample efficiency, scalability, and generalisation, which have expanded their use in a variety of fields, including robotics, healthcare, finance, and autonomous systems. Interdisciplinary methods have further improved RL research by incorporating insightful ideas from control theory, economics, psychology, and neurology. It is clear from looking back at the development of RL that the subject has advanced from simple theoretical ideas to complex, practical implementations. Technological advancements in computing power, innovative algorithms, and a deeper understanding of learning processes have all contributed to this expansion. The emergence of quantum computing and its integration with other AI paradigms represent promising new directions that should push the limits of what RL is capable of. The future of RL appears to be both promising and challenging. Prospective advances in hybrid systems and quantum-enhanced RL have the potential to transform the solving of complex problems. Nonetheless, unanswered concerns remain regarding the ethical use of RL systems, ensuring reliable and fair decision-making, and optimizing learning algorithms. RL has made significant progress, with its applications expanding across a wide range of industries. The development of more sample-efficient algorithms that leverage methods, such as model-based RL, meta-learning, and transfer learning, is a significant trend. Their application to complex tasks, such as autonomous driving, robotic manipulation, and language-based interactions, has increased with the integration of RL with other AI paradigms, including computer vision and natural language processing. The importance of ethical and societal factors has increased, with a growing emphasis on ensuring that RL systems are accountable, transparent, and fair.

Furthermore, by more effectively handling complicated issues, the nexus of RL with quantum computing offers a frontier that might completely transform the discipline. Innovations in algorithms and enhancements in processing capacity have propelled the progression of RL from theoretical foundations to real-world applications. Initially, Virtual Reality (VR) was primarily used in games and simulations, but it has since evolved to encompass more complex and dynamic real-world contexts.

Looking ahead, RL presents both great opportunities and significant challenges. Developing RL agents with the ability to learn continuously, adapt, and improve without periodic retraining is one of the primary goals. More effective exploration techniques and advances in self-supervised learning are needed to achieve this. To ensure that RL has a beneficial influence on society and that its full potential is realised, addressing these challenges will be imperative.

Exploring the Interplay between Reinforcement Learning and Human Decision-Making: A Multidisciplinary Perspective



Vinay Kumar1, *, Banalaxmi Brahma1, Surendra Solanki2
1 Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Dr. B.R. Ambedkar National Institute of Technology Jalandhar, Jalandhar, Punjab, India
2 Department of Artificial Intelligence & Machine Learning, Manipal University Jaipur, Jaipur, Rajasthan, India

Abstract

Reinforcement learning is a type of machine learning in which an agent picks up knowledge from its surroundings and past performance. It is a potent machine learning method in which decisions are guided by feedback. The decision-making process requires the evaluation of all the possible options and then selecting the best option that is aligned with the requirement. Human decision-making is influenced by many factors, like experience, time, risk, emotions, and uncertainty. In the case of reinforcement learning and human decision-making, both RL agents and humans learn from past experience. RL agents and humans learn from the trial-and-error method, whereas humans in decision-making learn from mental models. The proposed chapter discusses the integration of reinforcement learning and human decision-making and explores the new capabilities of integrating these two growing areas. It also examines how each area influences the other when they are integrated.

Keywords: Analytical hierarchy process, Deep Q neural network, Human decision making, Iowa gambling task, Reinforcement learning, State-action- reward-state-action.

* Corresponding author Vinay Kumar: Department of Computer Science and Engineering, Dr. B.R. Ambedkar National Institute of Technology Jalandhar, Jalandhar, Punjab, India; E-mail: vinayk.cs.23@nitj.ac.in

INTRODUCTION

Decision-making is a skill used in both daily life and computer science. Every decision has many options, so first, it is required to go through all available options and then select the best option.

Human decision-making is influenced by many factors, like experience, time, risk, emotions, and uncertainty [1]. One kind of machine learning is Reinforcement Learning (RL), in which an agent picks up knowledge from its surroundings and past performance. A potent machine learning method called reinforcement learning uses feedback to guide decision-making. Maximizing rewards through incremental decision-making is the primary goal of reinforcement learning [2]. Like reinforcement learning agents, humans learn from past experiences and feedback from the environment. Reinforcement learning agents depend upon experience-based learning, whereas humans use a mental model, which is a combination of observation, experience, and knowledge. A reinforcement learning agent and a human both make decisions in the environment to maximize their benefits. Reinforcement learning models are very helpful in understanding the cognitive process of decision-making [1]. The author provides a quick overview of human decision-making and reinforcement learning in this chapter. The integration of these two fields is then further discussed using a decision-making model that utilizes reinforcement learning.

Machine Learning

Machine learning is an area of computer science where the main objective is for machines to learn automatically with the help of past data without being explicitly programmed. There are four machine learning classifications, which are given in Fig. (1).

Fig. (1))
Classification of machine learning.
  1. Supervised Machine Learning: In this learning classification, machines are trained using labelled data (training data) and then machines predict about new data based on the training.
  2. Unsupervised Machine Learning: Using an unlabeled dataset, unsupervised machine learning trains the system, allowing it to generate predictions unsupervised.
  3. Semi-Supervised Machine Learning: In this type of machine learning, labeled and unlabeled data are used to train the machines.
  4. Reinforcement Learning: It uses a machine learning method that is feedback-based. Here, an agent looks at the surroundings and the outcomes of actions to determine which action to take [3].

Reinforcement Learning

It uses a feedback-based machine learning method. Here, an agent looks at the environment and the outcomes of actions to determine which action to take. The agent can only learn by experience because there is no labeled data. The agent receives positive reinforcement or a reward for each action they perform correctly, and they receive negative reinforcement or a penalty for each erroneous action. In certain situations, such as chess games or robotics, where decisions must be made sequentially and the goal is long-term [4], reinforcement learning is employed to find solutions.

Framework of Reinforcement Learning: Let's first examine the reinforcement learning framework given in Fig. (2), which has several crucial terms:

Fig. (2))
Framework of reinforcement learning.
  • Agent: represents an object whose goal is to learn a strategy to optimize a certain process.
  • Environment: acts as a world in which the agent is located and consists of a set of different states.
  • Action: Movements performed by the agent in the environment are known as actions.
  • State: Every action the agent takes results in a state return from the environment.
  • Reward: To evaluate the actions of the agents, the environment provided feedback to the agents.
  • Policy: It is a strategic guide for agents, helping them determine their next move based on the current circumstances.
  • Value: It contrasts with immediate satisfaction and is designed to be tailored for the future by considering the discount factor.
  • Q-value: It closely resembles the value. However, it mandates an additional parameter in the form of the current action.

At each timestamp, the agent can perform an action in the environment that will change the environment’s state to a new one. Additionally, the agent will receive feedback indicating how good or bad the chosen action was. This feedback is called a reward and is represented in numerical form. Over time, the agent eventually learns the best strategy to maximize the total reward by utilizing feedback from various states and actions.

Elements of Reinforcement Learning

  • Policy (π): In specific situations, the agent's behavior is determined by the policy. In essence, the policy assigns actions to states. It could be a search process, a function, or a lookup table. The primary objective of the reinforcement technique is to determine which technique is most effective.
  • Reward Signal (R): The measure assesses the quality of an event and outlines the goal of the problem, which the agent then utilizes to optimize the overall reward. Consequently, the main driver for policy updates is the reward. The reward may be given out immediately or later; in the latter instance, the agent will need to decide which actions are most pertinent to a later payout [5, 6].
  • Value Function: Utilizing the value function, which forecasts the entire future reward, one may assess the states and make appropriate action decisions. The expected return when beginning from a state s is represented by the state-value function V(s) is presented in equation (1):
(1)

The entire rewards R from time-step t are represented by the return Gt [5, 6], which is the total of the discounted future reward plus the current reward. Presented in equation (2):

(2)

When future rewards are estimated at today, the discount indicates how much they have degraded. Its value ranges from 0 to 1. However, there is substantial debate regarding the use of discounts.

From a state s, the action-value pair q (s,a) represents the predicted return of carrying out an action a [5, 6]. The mathematical formulation is presented in equation (3):

(3)
  • Model of the Environment:The environment model enables predictions about how the environment will behave. Model-based methods, on the other hand, employ models and planning; models themselves are an optional component of reinforcement learning. Conversely, model-free approaches do not require the agent to have a model of its surroundings. Model-free methods' learning procedure is obviously empirical.

REINFORCEMENT LEARNING ALGORITHMS

Reinforcement learning algorithms are either Model-based or model-free reinforcement learning algorithms. In a model-based reinforcement learning algorithm, the agent has a model that helps to understand how the environment behaves. So, the model helps the agent to make decisions. Whereas, model-free approaches do not require the agent to have a model of its surroundings. So, the agent learns from experience by the trial-and-error method. Model-free reinforcement learning algorithms are either policy-based or value-based learning algorithms. Value-based learning algorithms focus on learning a value function. This function calculates the predicted reward an agent will receive in the future for existing in a certain state (state value function) or acting in that state (state-action value function). Policy-based learning algorithms focus on learning a policy function. This function maps states to the probabilities of taking different actions. Reinforcement learning algorithms [3] are classified as per Fig. (3).

Fig. (3))
Classification of reinforcement learning algorithms.
  • Policy Gradient:There are two types of policies: deterministic and stochastic. A deterministic policy maps each state to a specific action, while a stochastic policy assigns a probability distribution over possible actions in a given state. Agents are computer programs that behave as decision-making and learning machines. The environment reacts to the interactions of the agent by providing incentives and indicating the possibility of the future state dispersing upon the agent's activities. The reward may be favorable or unfavorable depending on the behavior. The distribution of probabilities of actions is presented by the algorithm policy(π), which has a parameter θ [3].
  • Trust Region Policy Optimization (TRPO): The method works with big, nonlinear policies, such as neural networks, to optimize them. TRPO provides steady progress with minimal hyperparameter adjustment. The iterative process is used to determine the maximum step size that can be taken within the trust regions to reach the global maximum. This leads to an updated policy that may be applied for additional training.
  • Proximal Policy Optimization (PPO): It gives outstanding performance in several difficult problems. Optimal trust region policy serves as an inspiration for this algorithm. At every step, the algorithm tries to compute a new policy to minimize the cost, ensuring that the departure from the previous policy is as small as possible [3].
  • Asynchronous Advantage Actor-Critic (A3C) is a novel technique created by DeepMind of Google that combines two ideas.
    • Asynchronous: It functions like a human colony, where everyone exchanges knowledge. Consequently, every agent obtains a variety of experiences in terms of decreased returns, which can be utilized for additional training.
    • Actor-critic: It merges the benefits of the value iteration strategy with the policy-iteration method, as in Q-learning.
  • Temporal Difference (TD): In this scenario, the agent gains the ability to forecast the future value of a variable and uses the obtained state values to lead upcoming actions that transform the condition of the environment. It is similar to dynamic programming and involves using the most recent value function estimates for bootstrapping and updating based on current estimates. Additionally, the algorithm resembles the Monte Carlo method in that it learns from sequences of experiences.
  • Q-learning: The letter “Q” denotes quality. A particular action's likelihood of yielding the best future reward is represented by the algorithm. The Q-table serves as a guide for the agent to select the best action for each state. By selecting the most optimal course of action (state, activity), it helps to increase expected rewards and restores the activity's typical potential compensation at that state. The Bellman equation is used to iteratively refresh the Q (s,a) [2, 3].
  • State-Action-Reward-State-Action (SARSA): SARSA algorithms diverge from Q-learning by not requiring the maximum reward for the next state when updating the Q-value in the table.
  • Deep Q Neural Network (DQN): As part of the procedure, a matrix is created to assist the agent in selecting the course of action that will maximize its future benefits. But creating a Q-table gets harder and takes longer as there are more states and actions in the surroundings. The Q-value for each potential action in deep Q-learning is produced by the input given to the system. The Epsilon-Greedy Exploration technique depends on the highest Q-network output to determine the next action. The Bellman equation is used to adjust the network weights and account for the uncertainty of the target value.
  • Imagination-Augmented Agents (I2A): Using forecasts and predictions similar to those found in deep policy networks, it learns to interpret predictions outside the area to construct plans in diverse formats. The approach has been shown to significantly improve data robustness, performance, and efficiency.
  • Model-Based Priors for Model-Free: It works like a link between reinforcement learning based on models and without them.

APPLICATIONS OF REINFORCEMENT LEARNING

  • Medical Imaging: Medical imaging can accurately detect diseases, but there is a risk of inaccuracy in the interpretation of the images, particularly when diagnosing cancer. Reinforcement learning methods have successfully addressed a variety of difficult problems in fields such as bioinformatics, medical diagnosis, speech recognition, language identification, computer vision, and robotic motion.
  • Robotics: Reinforcement learning is commonly utilized in the robotics sector for a range of applications such as flexible manipulation, medical assistance, reducing complexity, validating models, identifying vulnerabilities, discretizing state actions, estimating task durations, cloud-based demonstrations, managing tasks, coordinating research efforts, optimizing productivity through simulated testing, and developing predictive models. The calculations of reinforcement learning algorithms are typically test-escalated, which poses a risk to the robot simulation.
  • Games: Software strategies for new games are being created and provided by deep reinforcement learning. Certain video games, including Atari, Chess, Minecraft, and Sudoku, are very challenging for people to learn and have gained popularity. Agents that use reinforcement learning learn by making mistakes [7].
  • Business Management: Reward learning is most likely used in recommender systems and business applications. Netflix uses reinforcement learning to recommend movies and TV shows to its users. Reinforcement algorithms are being used by Spotify to handle track abuse and investigation. Warehouse managers employ algorithms to maximize space usage, while online retailers use them to study consumer behavior and customize items and customer experiences.
  • Hyperparameter Selection for Neural Networks: An iterative process of trial and error is used to determine the neural network architecture and hyperparameter selection. Therefore, it can be expressed as a problem involving reinforcement learning.

HUMAN DECISION MAKING

Humans receive information through their sense organs, which are subsequently processed and interpreted by the attention and working memory function of the brain [8]. Focus is directed on pertinent details within the large sensory stream in the case of attention function. Working memory serves as a makeshift workspace where selected data is kept for processing. Humans are dependent on mental models, which are internal representations of the world constructed from prior knowledge and experiences. Humans use mental models to make decisions by considering all of their possibilities and choosing the one that has the best chance of producing the desired result. Mental model bias has the potential to influence decision-making. The process of making decisions is also influenced by intuition and emotions. When making decisions, humans consider all of their options, trusting their gut feelings and intuition as well as weighing the advantages and disadvantages of each choice before choosing a course of action. Making decisions involves ongoing learning. Humans are lifelong learners from the decisions we make. These results revise mental models and impact judgments in the future [9]. Humans can gain better decision-making abilities and adjust to new circumstances because of this feedback loop.

Each decision-making problem has a unique set of options that must be analyzed to determine which is best. Selecting might require a lot of work and time to identify the best option because there are many factors at play. A few significant factors [1] are emotions, time, uncertainty, risk, past experiences, and the surrounding environment, given in Fig. (4).

Fig. (4))
Influencing factors for decision making.

With the help of past experiences, humans evaluate all possible options for decision-making and then select the best one from them. Emotions could be positive or negative. Humans select the same alternative again if emotions are positive, and if emotions are negative, they try to reject the previous bad alternatives. When people are forced to make decisions within a constrained time frame, Human Decision-Making (HDM) could be challenging. Decision-making is also affected by uncertainty. Making decisions is challenging when future results are unknown. Every factor has a distinct importance in HDM. However, it's not always feasible to take into account every factor while making judgments [1].

Human Decision Making and Reinforcement Learning

One of the most well-liked learning games is the Iowa Gambling Task (IGT), which is based on participants' prior experiences. Originally, the game is played with actual cards. In other studies, a computerized version of IGT is employed, using virtual cards instead of actual cards. One deck at a time selection is required of the participants, who may choose to get a reward or a penalty. Each member receives a virtual loan of $2000 at the start. The value of the loan money is increased or decreased based on how much they win or lose. The participants are not provided with information regarding the payoff schedule or the overall number of trials. All they must do is play the game and take home the biggest reward [1].

Literature Review on Human Decision-Making and Reinforcement Learning

A brief detail about the literature review is displayed in Fig. (5). Premkumar et al. (2008) employed a blend of Reinforcement Learning (RL) and Expectancy Valence (EV) models to explore the cognitive processes at play in Decision Making [10]. In one research study, researchers used the Prospect Valence Learning (PVL) model to address the drawbacks of the EV model. Compared to the EV model, the PVL model performs better [11]. Information regarding 44 students who are in good health was utilized, and the effects of generated moods on participants' DM behavior were investigated by Rasmussen et al., (2019) [12] and found that the positive-induced mood group demonstrated superior performance on the IGT compared to the negative and neutral groups, based on the data. Levine et al. (2005) created a supplementary model focused on emotions to assess cognitive impairments in individuals with damage to the orbital prefrontal cortex [13]. The study also looked at how artificial time constraint affects decision-making behavior and learning using HDM modeling and IGT. According to a study by L. Schutte (2012), individuals tend to process information at a faster rate and experience heightened agitation when under pressure to meet deadlines [14].

Fig. (5))
Brief literature review.

Various studies have employed factor modelling to comprehend the HDM cognitive process and its impact on participants' attitudes and behaviour. However, no study has yet been discovered that incorporates every crucial element into a model. Apart from the individual factors that influence decision-making, there exist other factors that impact the decision-making process either directly or indirectly. As a result, a computational model for HDM that incorporates several important human factors is required. Determining the significance of every human factor is now necessary, which is another difficult challenge. Due to its ability to facilitate pairwise comparison, the Analytical Hierarchy Process (AHP) is used to assign weight to each human factor among factors or standards. Here, human factors are also considered by setting priority values through the integration of AHP with the created HDM computational model. Furthermore, upon integrating all DM factors into a model, an analysis was conducted on the IGT learning and its corresponding DM factors [1].

HUMAN DECISION-MAKING MODEL BASED ON RL

Gupta et al. (2022) considered three presumptions to form the basis for the creation of an HDM computational model [15]. They are TDC/TIC rules, DRI, and utility functions. These functions are combined in different ways to create four learning models. The initial factor to consider is the utility function, which computes the anticipated value of the result. Prospect Utility (PU) and Expected Utility (EU) functions are the two additional categories into which the utility function is separated. Risk, uncertainty, and probability-related decision problems are addressed by the PU theory [16]. This psychological theory explains how people make decisions in these kinds of circumstances. Conversely, according to the EU theory, individuals are expected to select an action that maximizes their utility, considering the likelihood of success. It is often utilized in cases where the future is uncertain and acts as a guide for addressing decision-making problems involving uncertainty. EU and PU are calculated using equations (4) and (5), respectively [1].

(4)

Here, y is the utility function's curvature and EU(t) is the expected utility for the current trial t. The weights assigned to profit and loss values are W and L. The amounts won or lost during trial t's deck selection are denoted by win(t) and loss(t).

(5)

The PU value for the current trial, t, is represented by PU(t), the utility function's form, α, and the loss aversion value, λ. x(t) = win(t) - |loss(t)| in this case. If x(t) – 0, it indicates a loss; if not, it indicates a gain.

The Decay RL (DRI) rule comes next. The selected alternative's expectancy-value is reduced by a specific amount in this rule, while the unselected alternative's expectancy-value is recalculated using equation (6). αkt and βkt = learning weightage provided to the past expectancy Ek (t-1).

The significance of having sensitivity θ(t) in TDC varies as one learns. In the case of TIC, on the other hand, the consistency value is independent of trial t and is fixed during the time interval specified by θ(t). Following model development, the models' outcomes are assessed using the simulation approach by looking at the value of Mean Square Deviation (MSD), which is outlined in equation (12) and validated by equation (13).

(12)

Here, n and k are the total number of decks and trials, respectively. The mean likelihood of selecting both simulated and experimental data on trial t is denoted by Dsim,k and Dexp,k.

For simulated data, Pr[D(t) = k] the average likelihood of each person, and N represents the overall number of participants.

The difference between observed or anticipated data and actual data is called the MSD value, and it is computed using a simulation method. To create projected probabilities, the average value for everyone was determined. A smaller MSD value suggests that the model is more accurate.

The primary aim of this study is to create a Reinforcement learning-based HDM computational model that combines various human factors such as past experiences, time, uncertainty, and emotions into one cohesive model. The priority of the different factors is determined using the AHP methodology in order to incorporate them into the model [1]. The established model defines the priorities based on their weighted value. Adding various human factors to the learning models creates a new equation to update the EV of prior outcomes. It can be found using Equation (14):

Equation (6)'s parameter is swapped out by a weighted parameter based on prior experience, in Equation (14). Equation (7) assigns the learning rate parameter () to . According to Yechiam and Busemeyer (2005) [17], the parameter of learning rate () provides a certain level of effectiveness in the connection between reinforcement learning and past experiences. In equation (8), the dummy variable is substituted for , which is paired with the uncertainty parameter, . If deck k is chosen, the value of = 1; otherwise, 0.

Handling decision difficulties in a multi-criteria or factors setting is the main goal of AHP. As a result, they split the problems into levels of alternatives so that each aim and option may be compared in pairs. The subproblems are first subjectively evaluated before being numerically valued using Saaty's fundamental scale. To determine the priority values, each alternative was assigned a unique rating based on those numerical scales. This approach involves comparing pairs and applying a set of principles to construct matrices that rank the alternatives and criteria. The decision problem is broken down into a hierarchy level [1] in Fig. (6). The main goal of this chapter is to discuss an RL-based HDM model that incorporates several human aspects. Various aspects concerning humans are considered distinct criteria that must be incorporated into a refined model built on user preferences.

The created HDM model's sequence flow [1] is displayed in Fig. (7). IGT data served as the input for the RL-based model that was created. The three components of the developed model are the choosing rule, learning, and utility functions. Four learning models are developed using EU/PU, DRI, and TDC/TIC in pairs and combinations.

Fig. (6))
Decision problem decomposition into hierarchical levels using AHP.
Fig. (7))
Sequence flow of decision-making model utilizing reinforcement learning.

AHP is used to calculate the priority worth for several factors related to human utilizing four different pair-wise matrices. One factor was given much weight in these matrices over another, and their corresponding values were computed. The suggested learning model received the computed weight value, and its MSD value was determined at various simulation values.

ETHICAL AND SOCIAL IMPLICATIONS

Some ethical and social implications are explained below:

  • Who is responsible for a bad decision made by a Reinforcement learning-driven system: the RL agent, the data sources, or the programmers? To ensure responsible development and deployment, it is imperative to establish unambiguous chains of accountability [18].
  • Even when working with RL agents, human monitoring of essential decision-making processes must be maintained. When necessary, humans must be able to step in and override RL judgments [19].
  • As RL agents develop in sophistication, they might replace humans in certain tasks. Retraining and reskilling strategies are crucial to preventing job loss and guaranteeing a smooth transition in society.

FUTURE SCOPE AND CHALLENGES

There are some future scopes explained for the integration of Reinforcement Learning and Human decision-making:

  • Shared Learning Frameworks: There is great promise in creating frameworks that allow humans and RL agents to learn from one another in real-time. This might entail giving RL agents advice and feedback from humans as they learn, which would speed up adaptation and provide stronger decision-making techniques [20].
  • Diverse Datasets and Human Oversight: It is crucial to train RL agents using a variety of representative datasets. Furthermore, human oversight in crucial decision-making procedures helps guarantee impartial results and lessen the influence of any biases [21].

There are some challenges explained for the reinforcement learning algorithm:

  • Safety and Security: Maintaining the safety and security of these systems is crucial as RL agents get more advanced. Strong security measures and fail-safe procedures need to be included to reduce any possible hazards related to RL algorithms used in real-world applications [22].
  • Scalability and Sample Efficiency: It is still difficult to scale reinforcement learning systems to solve intricate real-world issues involving large volumes of data. Furthermore, increasing RL agents' sample efficiency, their capacity to learn from less data, is essential for broader adoption [23].

CONCLUSION

In this chapter, a decision-making model based on reinforcement learning is discussed, which tries to integrate many human factors as experience, emotions, risk, and time into a single framework to better understand the human decision-making process. The Analytic Hierarchy Process (AHP) is used to prioritize these factors.

Reinforcement Learning and Human Decision Making both require negotiating unknowns, coming to decisions, and taking lessons from past mistakes. We may explore fascinating opportunities for improving AI and deepening our understanding of human cognition by combining these two potent forces. Here are the main conclusions:

  • Collaboration and Similarities: Reward systems, value estimation, and a delicate balance between exploration and exploitation are all used in both RL and human decision making. This provides opportunities for RL-driven decision support systems and human-in-the-loop learning.
  • Moral Aspects to Consider: Priorities include bias, fairness, transparency, and safety. We need to create RL algorithms that are understandable, reduce bias in training sets, and give human judgment precedence when making crucial decisions.
  • Prospective Research Areas: Human-AI collaboration in intricate settings, fairness-aware reinforcement learning, and shared learning frameworks are all interesting areas to pursue. It is still imperative to address issues with scalability, sampling efficiency, and safety.
